              Luke 24:43 [319] doesn't appear to have any textual variants, meaning that there is no manuscript evidence of a later addition or corruption to this verse.

                1.3.8.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                The originals themselves may contain errors, which were then faithfully transmitted to later copies.

              1.3.8.1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
              This episode doesn't appear in any of the other gospels or in the epistles.

          1.3.8.1.1.2. Pro:
          In Matthew 28:9 [320] (John 20:17 [321] may be related) the women clasp Jesus's feet, which shouldn't be possible for a hallucination.

            1.3.8.1.1.2.1. Pro:
            Matthew 28:9 was probably not fabricated because the author, Matthew, was martyred [322].

              1.3.8.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
              The author of Matthew himself may not have fabricated it but rather only passed on what someone else had fabricated.

              1.3.8.1.1.2.1.2. Con:
              The gospel of Matthew wasn't written by Matthew the apostle of Jesus.

                1.3.8.1.1.2.1.2.1. Pro:
                Matthew was not actually written by Matthew.

          1.3.8.1.1.3. Pro:
          In John 20:24-29 [323] Thomas puts his hands in Jesus' wounds, which shouldn't be possible for a hallucination.

            1.3.8.1.1.3.1. Pro:
            Grief hallucinations can be auditory or visual but the source [324] doesn't give any evidence they can be tactile.

          1.3.8.1.1.4. Pro:
          In John 21:13 [325] Jesus hands his disciples bread and fish, which a hallucination shouldn't be able to do.

            1.3.8.1.1.4.1. Pro:
            -> See 1.3.8.1.1.3.1.

          1.3.8.1.1.5. Con:
          The episodes in the New Testament that attempt to show it wasn't just an hallucination (e.g. they touch his body or he interacts with the physical world) would then be legends or fabrications.

            1.3.8.1.1.5.1. Con:
            The accounts began showing up too soon after the death of Jesus for such legends to have developed in that time.

              1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1. Pro:
              Luke was probably written [326] less than 60 years after the crucifixion.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1. Con:
                60 years is plenty of time for stories like these to arise.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.1. Con:
                  We have a rolling history from 60 years ago. We usually have living members of eyewitnesses over that time period, so even with plenty of time, we do not regularly see "legends" arise.

                    1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                    In cases where few witnesses were present to observe something, that many other people were alive at the same time is irrelevant if they have no information that would disconfirm what the few people witnessed.

                    1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.1.2. Con:
                    In some cases, the memory and experiences of those who claim otherwise is labeled as invalid or hostile and shunned or attacked.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.2. Con:
                  The George Washington cherry tree legend first [327] appeared in 1806, 68 years after it would have happened in 1738 [328] (6 years plus his birthday in 1732).

                    1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
                    This wasn't an organically developed legend but a story invented from whole cloth by a single individual (Mason Locke Weems [329]).

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.3. Pro:
                  Qanon arose within 5 years.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4. Pro:
                  Mormonism arose within several years.

                    1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1. Con:
                    The legends within Mormonism came about many centuries after they allegedly took place in the Americas.

                      1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1. Con:
                      That's not relevant: the legends went from totally unknown to widely believed within the Mormon community.

                        1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1. Con:
                        This is relevant, because the reason for legends requiring a large time gap is that without a sufficient time gap between when the event supposedly happened and the legend formed, there will be eyewitnesses who can contradict the legend and give the true account.

                          1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1. Con:
                          In both the cases of the post resurrection appearances and the golden tablets or visit of Moroni, the alleged events happened in private where only a select few could see them. Thus, in either case, there wasn't any chance for disconfirmatory witnesses.

                            1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                            Many of the resurrection witnesses were martyred for their testimony and given the chance to recant, unlike the golden tablets/Moroni witnesses.

                              1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                              Mormons were persecuted [330] for their beliefs.

                                1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                                They didn't endure martyrdom, which goes far beyond other forms of persecution.

                                1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1.1.1.2. Con:
                                Although Joseph Smith was murdered, it wasn't done in such a way that he could have saved himself by recanting.

                                  1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
                                  Joseph Smith knew he would be murdered and yet he made the choice to voluntarily surrender to authorities and go to Carthage jail.

                                    1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
                                    Joseph Smith said to the company who were with him when he made the decision to surrender [331], "I am going like a lamb to the slaughter, but I am as calm as a summer's morning. I have a consciences void of an offense toward God and toward all men. If they take my life, I shall die an innocent man, and my blood shall cry from the ground for vengeance, and it shall be said of me, 'He was murdered in cold blood!'"

                            1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1.2. Con:
                            Jesus is described appearing in public to many people after his resurrection.

                              1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1.2.1. Pro:
                              In Matthew 28:16-17 [332] the disciples meet the resurrected Jesus outside on a mountain.

                              1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1.2.2. Pro:
                              According to 1 Corinthians 15:6 [333] Jesus appeared to over 500 people.

                              1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.4.1.1.1.1.2.3. Con:
                              These were legends.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.1.1.1.5. Pro:
                  Urban legends [334] commonly arise over short times.

            1.3.8.1.1.5.2. Con:
            If the accounts were legends, there wouldn't have been enough time before the gospels were written for them to grow to the point of completely replacing the real series of events in tellings of the story.

              1.3.8.1.1.5.2.1. Con:
              There could have been enough time.

              1.3.8.1.1.5.2.2. Con:
              They didn't so much replace the real elements of the story as fill in what was not known in the story.

            1.3.8.1.1.5.3. Pro:
            These accounts are likely legends because the authors were strongly biased.

            1.3.8.1.1.5.4. Pro:
            These accounts were likely legends because competing groups at the time would not have devoted time to criticizing the accounts, and thus legendary embellishments could have gone undetected.

              1.3.8.1.1.5.4.1. Con:
              Early, motivated, critics of Christianity existed.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.4.1.1. Pro:
                Porphyry of Tyre wrote a book titled Against the Christians [335] around 275-300 AD attacking Christianity.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.4.1.2. Pro:
                Celsus wrote a book titled The True Word [336] around 170-180 AD against Christianity.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.4.1.3. Con:
                The examples given were all written after the NT texts were already written, and so cannot possibly have influenced them.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.4.1.4. Pro:
                The New Testament writings already show evidence of Christians trying to counter the claims of Gnostics, which means there must have been Gnostics criticizing Christianity already.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.4.1.4.1. Pro:
                  1 John 4:2-3 [337] and 2 John 7 [338] appear to be countering Gnostic claims supporting docetism [339], the idea that Jesus didn't really have a physical body.

                    1.3.8.1.1.5.4.1.4.1.1. Pro:
                    In 1 John 4:1 these Gnostics are described as "false prophets", which means they must already be trying to spread their ideas to others.

              1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2. Pro:
              The NT was not written to be read by non-Christians but would primarily have been read by Christians to Christians or to people being evangelized. As Christianity was a small and insignificant sect until the second or third centuries, critics would have no reason to pick apart the gospel narratives.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.1. Pro:
                The NT was written entirely in Koine Greek and thus would have been spread throughout the Roman empire to young churches, mostly among people who had never heard of Christianity before, who had no background knowledge of 1st century Judea, and who likely already believed in several legendary stories themselves.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2. Con:
                Gnostic authors appear to have been aware of the NT, since they cited [340] passages in it.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1. Con:
                  Gnostics were indistinguishable as merely a subculture of Christianity, with a more mystical or abstract understanding of the resurrection, until they were condemned as heretics and ousted from Christian communities in the third century.

                    1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1. Con:
                    Despite this, the Gnostics would have been motivated to pick apart some of the most important gospel passages for establishing the historicity of the resurrection, such as Jesus being touched by the disciples after his resurrection.

                      1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1.1. Con:
                      Even if they did offer refutations of the physical resurrection, their rebuttals were likely destroyed along with their other writings.

                        1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1.1.1. Con:
                        Rebuttals that Christians tried to respond to would have remained in Christian literature.

                          1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                          Not necessarily: it's possible rebuttals once existed that are now lost to history.

                        1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1.1.2. Con:
                        Gnostic literature was preserved in the Nag Hammadi collection [341].

                          1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
                          The Nag Hammadi library represents only a fraction of all gnostic works: some of the texts in the library were themselves destroyed.

                          1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1.1.2.2. Pro:
                          There are over 50 texts [342] in the collection, compared to the 66 books in the Protestant Bible. This means the Nag Hammadi collection represents a significant amount of Gnostic writings.

                            1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1.1.2.2.1. Con:
                            There were many more gnostic texts that were not in the recovered Nag Hammadi library.

                              1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1.1.2.2.1.1. Pro:
                              The Gospel of Basilides [343] is a lost gnostic text.

                              1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1.1.2.2.1.2. Pro:
                              Many Valentinian texts [344] are lost or exist only in fragments.

                      1.3.8.1.1.5.4.2.2.1.1.2. Con:
                      They were not motivated to pick apart the passages about the physical resurrection, to show that the resurrection wasn't physical, but rather were motivated to reinterpret them, which they did.

            1.3.8.1.1.5.5. Pro:
            The accounts differ about what happened after the crucifixion, suggesting these details are legendary.

              1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1. Con:
              The accounts agree on many important facts regarding what happened after the crucifixion.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1.1. Pro:
                Matthew, Luke, and John all agree that after the crucifixion people were able to touch Jesus and know that he was physical, which is the key point in this branch.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1.1.1. Con:
                  All the accounts of people interacting with Jesus' body are significantly different. This suggests the absence of any historical core to the stories, and rather various legends around a vague central narrative.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1.1.2. Con:
                  The fact that Paul and Mark, which were written significantly before the other gospels, never mention any such stories counts against the stories being otherwise than legends.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1.1.3. Con:
                  There is a clear motive for the authors to add that element, as it makes clear they believed Jesus rose in the flesh, rather than only in the spirit, as some early Christians believed. Thus, it functions as a polemic against those who only believed Jesus rose in the spirit.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1.2. Pro:
                All of the accounts agree that Jesus's tomb was found empty.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1.3. Pro:
                All the accounts agree that the empty tomb was discovered on the first day of the week.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1.4. Con:
                Where the accounts do not differ can be accounted for by several other factors.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1.4.1. Pro:
                  Some commonalities form a historical backbone.

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1.4.2. Pro:
                  Some commonalities arise from legends that arose and spread early (e.g. by single individuals like Paul).

                  1.3.8.1.1.5.5.1.4.3. Pro:
                  Some commonalities are explained by the authors copying from one another.

              1.3.8.1.1.5.5.2. Con:
              The differences between the accounts are plausibly due to different eyewitnesses witnessing different parts of what happened rather than due to them being legends.

            1.3.8.1.1.5.6. Con:
            The accounts of people physically touching the resurrected Jesus are probably not legends because they portray beloved apostles in an embarrassing manner.

              1.3.8.1.1.5.6.1. Pro:
              In one of the stories, John 20:24-29 [345], Thomas is portrayed as a doubter.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.6.1.1. Con:
                Being a doubter isn't embarrassing: it implies he cared about knowing the truth and wanted to make sure he wasn't being fooled.

              1.3.8.1.1.5.6.2. Pro:
              In Luke 24:25 [346] Jesus rebukes the disciples on the road to Emmaus as foolish and slow.

                1.3.8.1.1.5.6.2.1. Con:
                This isn't embarrassing but rather humbling and humanizing, and showing them to be inferior to Jesus. All of this is entirely expected within the framework of the early Church and its message.

        1.3.8.1.2. Con:
        Grief hallucinations do not typically lead to the belief that the deceased has been resurrected.

          1.3.8.1.2.1. Con:
          They would in this case since there was some expectation that Jesus was the Messiah and thus had to come back from the dead in some way so that he could return again.

            1.3.8.1.2.1.1. Con:
            The Jewish understanding of "Messiah" would not have likely inspired paranormal, mystical, magical, or deity-like behavior, since the literally meaning of the word was, "anointed one." The Jewish people anointed many people [347] and thus would have been more akin to a title like in the military, (private, sergeant, ...) then the precursor to Jesus being immortal.

              1.3.8.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
              Messiah didn't merely mean "anointed one", thought that is the literal meaning. The connotation is much more weighty, as the Messiah was the anticipated savior of the Jews, about whom many prophecies are given in the earlier prophets.

        1.3.8.1.3. Con:
        Many disciples would have had to have the same or very similar grief hallucinations, which is unlikely.

          1.3.8.1.3.1. Con:
          They need not have been more similar than involving Jesus: from the experience of Jesus, the explanation would then be that he rose from the dead.

            1.3.8.1.3.1.1. Con:
            According to testimony accounts of resurrection, Jesus appeared to over 500 people at one time [348], making any apparition version of Jesus, verifiable.

              1.3.8.1.3.1.1.1. Pro:
              Paul even invites the reader to verify his story by saying, "...most of whom are still living... [349]," giving any would be skeptics the ability to fact check the story.

              1.3.8.1.3.1.1.2. Con:
              This was a later interpolation.

              1.3.8.1.3.1.1.3. Con:
              This was a bluff that no one actually could check.

              1.3.8.1.3.1.1.4. Con:
              If this had really happened, other sources would mention it. As no other source mentions it, it likely never happened.

            1.3.8.1.3.1.2. Con:
            The hallucinations must not have been mutually contradictory; otherwise the disciples would have realized they were hallucinations.

              1.3.8.1.3.1.2.1. Con:
              Confirmation bias, motivated reasoning, and vagueness would eliminate any contradictions, if indeed there were any.

          1.3.8.1.3.2. Pro:
          -> See 1.3.8.1.3.1.2.

          1.3.8.1.3.3. Pro:
          Sometimes Jesus is described appearing to multiple disciples at once (e.g. John 20:19 [350]). Each disciple in one of these cases would plausibly have checked with the others that they had also experienced the same thing at the same time.

        1.3.8.1.4. Con:
        The grief hallucinations would not have suddenly stopped for all the disciples after 40 days (Acts 1:3, 9 [351]).

          1.3.8.1.4.1. Con:
          The point is only mentioned in Luke-Acts, which was written around 80 CE, making it of dubious veracity.

          1.3.8.1.4.2. Con:
          The appearances may not have stopped then. They may have stopped before or after that time. But to give a definite time, 40 days was used to give a firm cutoff.

            1.3.8.1.4.2.1. Pro:
            40 is a typological number, signifying "many"

      1.3.8.2. Pro:
      Jesus could have been substituted [352] with a doppelgänger [353], a twin, or a clone. Such scenarios would still be more plausible [354] than a supernatural [355] intervention by a creator deity to resurrect Jesus after he was dead for three days.

        1.3.8.2.1. Pro:
        Assuming his body was indeed in a known tomb that was subsequently empty, the body could have been removed – for example through a tunnel or merely due to insufficient observance of the tomb.

          1.3.8.2.1.1. Pro:
          The stolen body hypothesis [356] posits that the body was removed by humans [357] from his burial place and was already discussed in the Gospel of Matthew [358] (~AD 70-100).

        1.3.8.2.2. Pro:
        If one assumes Jesus really did seem to have been resurrected multiple days after his death without perception issues like hallucinations, then it was either a genuine biological revival such as by aliens [359] quickly connecting Jesus to a [360] life-sustaining [361] machine [362] or it only appeared like a 'resurrection' while the 'resurrected Jesus' was in actuality another being (with one of the three named natural explanations being true).

        1.3.8.2.3. Pro:
        While population size at the time of Jesus was substantially smaller [363], it is still [364] likely he had some lookalike 'doppelgänger [365]' and not unlikely that he or relevant people got into contact with them. Motivations for orchestrating the replacement by a lookalike could include maintaining the vitality of the religion at the time.

        1.3.8.2.4. Pro:
        The physical possibility of doppelgänger, lookalike [366] twins, and clones [367] is proven in contrast to the physical possibility of both a monotheistic creator-God intervening on Earth or the resurrection of humans several days after full-death. Because of that, the plausibility of naturalist substitution hypothesis explanations is larger than the plausibility of a super-natural Godly intervention.

        1.3.8.2.5. Con:
        Presbyterian minister, Dr. Barry Downing argued [368] Jesus himself was an extraterrestrial (or possibly of partly extraterrestrial origin) and that he had alien characteristics or powers which was used for an actual genuine revival instead of a substitution.

          1.3.8.2.5.1. Con:
          Jesus did not mention or attribute his resurrection to anything like extraterrestrial-origin beings [369], but to the Christian God.

        1.3.8.2.6. Con:
        The population size was much smaller in the past and communication was different. Thus, it would have been harder to find or come across a sufficiently similar-looking person to credibly substitute Jesus with a willing doppelgänger.

        1.3.8.2.7. Con:
        If Jesus had a twin [370] brother [371], that would have been known before the resurrection and by now and his step brothers would have known about the imposter [372].

        1.3.8.2.8. Pro:
        Jesus could also have been substituted before [373] rather than after the crucifixion where someone died on the cross, but it was not Jesus [374].

        1.3.8.2.9. Con:
        There is no evidence for any of these scenarios and it's not how the the Bible describes the events. The gospels wouldn't lie, or even fool followers by helping implement a substitution, and are to be trusted. Since the Bible is the word of God according to the Bible, the resurrection was genuine and due to Godly intervention.

    1.3.9. Pro:
    Many people who claim to have undergone an alien abduction claim to have seen what appear to be clones of humans [375] and according to abduction researcher Dr. Karla Turner, abductee Pat was shown [376] a Jesus imitation by an alien for better compliance and another abductee was shown how a clone of hers was going to work instead of her without anybody noticing. This indicates aliens have the ability to clone humans and to replace them without people noticing which is what could have been done here.

  1.4. Con:
  Jesus never died on the cross, instead he passed out and woke up again in his tomb

    1.4.1. Pro:
    Jesus' reluctance to be seen clearly by his followers immediately after the crucifixion makes sense if he merely survived and needed to find out which of his followers he could trust and which would hand him back to the authorities. Hence his behaviour on the road to Emmaus - listening and finding out what they thought then disappearing. If he had had supernatural insight he would have already known who he could trust

      1.4.1.1. Con:
      If Jesus did survive the crucifixion, he probably would have been in noticeably bad shape when he met the disciples on the road to Emmaus just a few days after his crucifixion (Luke 24:13-35 [377]). However, they don't seem to notice anything unusual about Jesus.

      1.4.1.2. Pro:
      The 'Road to Emmaus [378]' story is notable in that Jesus' followers (i) failed to recognise him and (ii) failed to notice the person they were talking to had just been severely wounded during the crucifixion. In contrast, a week later when Thomas saw Jesus [379] his wounds were clearly visible. A plausible explaination is that on the road to Emmaus Jesus was disguised (e.g. with loose wrap around clothing and a head covering) to prevent him from being identified. With Thomas he wanted the wounds to be seen

    1.4.2. Pro:
    Jesus didnt have to survive the crucifixion for long or make a complete recovery. He only had to be seen alive by sufficient numbers of the faithful to convince them that he was supernatural. The bible says Jesus 'disappeared' after 40 days. Perhaps after a few weeks in hiding he went off to pray alone and succumbed to his wounds. The disciples coundn't find him (perhaps an animal dragged the body away) and the story of the ascension emerged.

      1.4.2.1. Pro:
      The bible narratives of Jesus' appearance after the crucifixion are highly detailled and precise. Furthermore they highlight the human frailties and disbelief of the disciples - exactly as you would expect people to behave. This suggests they are an accurate witness account of what was observed. In contrast, the stories of the ascention are vague - suggesting they might be a post hoc rationalisation of what happened.

    1.4.3. Con:
    The fact that Jesus was speared in the side during his crucifixion makes his survival more unlikely.

      1.4.3.1. Con:
      The depiction of the spear wound in Jesus' side in Christian iconography is wrong [380]. It depicts a slit wound penetrating parallel with the ribs [381] and made by a two edged 'leaf' shaped spear head. Instead, the Roman soldiers would have been equipped with a pilum [382] which has a relatively small pyrimidal shaped head. It was designed to get stuck in shields. The mass of a pilum made it dangerous as a thrown weapon but would have limited its effectiveness when pocking someone above head height

        1.4.3.1.1. Con:
        The source says, "The pilum was also used as a melee weapon in close-quarters combat. [383]"

        1.4.3.1.2. Pro:
        The small tip of a pilum [384] (3/8 to 1/2 an inch wide) and heavy shaft were designed to penetrate armour and shields when used as a thrown weapon. Whilst the pilum could be used as a melee weapon (e.g. against cavalry) that was not its primary purpose. Jesus' spear wound might well have been a sharp prod to see if the victim responded rather than an intended lethal blow. Early medieval spear blades [385] (5 inches long by 2 inches wide) would have made a much worse chest wound

        1.4.3.1.3. Con:
        Even if the spear jab wouldn't have killed a normal person, it would have easily killed a weakened person who had been crucified for hours.

          1.4.3.1.3.1. Con:
          Jesus' survival did not have to be likely or probable. Only possible. Even a tiny chance of physical survival is more plausible than a once in the history of the cosmos instance of divine intervention. Unlikely things happen - people win the lottery despite the odds being tens of millions to one against

            1.4.3.1.3.1.1. Con:
            The challenge with presenting this as only needing it to be possible, is that there are no instances of a person surviving a Roman execution of this type. This would disprove the possibility since this method of punishment isn't used anymore.

            1.4.3.1.3.1.2. Con:
            Under the Christian view this is not a "once in the history of the cosmos instance of divine intervention." There have been other miracles.

              1.4.3.1.3.1.2.1. Pro:
              Under the Christian view Moses split the Red Sea.

      1.4.3.2. Con:
      This may not be a fact: it is only mentioned in John, the latest of the gospels. It may be a fabrication: It may hold a symbolic significance or may be a way to make clear that Jesus was dead, which isn't as clear in the other gospels.

    1.4.4. Pro:
    Christianity is the only world religion based not on a belief in an infallible god but on a belief in infallible squaddies, Under normal circumstances, if numerous credible witnesses reported that someone had been seen alive after their supposed execution you would think of human rather than supernatural explanations

      1.4.4.1. Pro:
      Imagine the board of enquiry if Jesus' "execution and ressurection" were to happen today. Your soldiers were ordered to tie an unarmed man to a post and shoot him repeatedly with a heavy machine gun. Then call in an airstrike to drop a 2000lb laser guided bomb on him. Then drive over the remains with a tank. Hmm ... what could possibly go wrong? And yet large numbers of credible witnesses claim to have seen him alive...

      1.4.4.2. Pro:
      Ordinary soldiers make mistakes and at the time Jesus was no one special. Immediately before a public holiday the soldiers and officials were probably in a hurry and simply made a mistake

    1.4.5. Pro:
    Seemingly impossible instances of human survival are known to occur

      1.4.5.1. Pro:
      Nicholas Stephen Alkemade [386] (10 December 1922 – 22 June 1987) was an English tail gunner in the Royal Air Force during World War II who survived a freefall of 18,000 feet (5,490 m) without a parachute when abandoning his out-of-control, burning Avro Lancaster heavy bomber over Germany.

        1.4.5.1.1. Con:
        Important medical advances were available during WWII but not in the first century.

          1.4.5.1.1.1. Pro:
          Penicillin [387] was discovered in 1928 and first used in 1942.

          1.4.5.1.1.2. Pro:
          Vaccines [388] were already available by WWII.

          1.4.5.1.1.3. Pro:
          Blood transfusions [389] were available by WWII.

      1.4.5.2. Pro:
      Alan Magee [390] was thrown clear from a flack-damaged B-17 bomber, fell over four miles before crashing through the glass roof of St Nazaire railway station. The glass roof shattered, mitigating the force of Magee's impact. He was taken prisoner of war and given medical treatment. He had 28 shrapnel wounds in addition to his injuries from the fall: several broken bones, severe damage to his nose and eye, lung and kidney damage, and a nearly severed right arm but survived until 2003.

        1.4.5.2.1. Con:
        Unlike Jesus, Magee received medical treatment by his captors according to the source.

      1.4.5.3. Pro:
      In May 1941 the British battle cruiser HMS Hood [391] was sunk when one or more 15 inch shells from Bismarck's fifth salvo detonated one of the Hood’s magazines. This initiated an almost instantaneous sequence of secondary explosions which broke the Hood's back and ripped apart the stern half of the ship. The remains of the Hood sank in 3 minutes with 1418 men on board. Non-the-less three men were thrown clear and rescued by the destroyer HMS Electra after 2 hours in the water

    1.4.6. Con:
    If the tomb was sealed, Jesus would have suffocated in it even if he survived the crucifixion.

      1.4.6.1. Con:
      The seal would not have to be 'air tight' in a modern sense.

    1.4.7. Con:
    Jesus's tomb was guarded, which means that he would have been caught if he survived the crucifixion and tried to escape [392].

      1.4.7.1. Pro:
      Jesus's tomb was guarded (linked claim).

        1.4.7.1.1. Con:
        Jesus was no one particularly special at the time. Even if guards were posted at the beginning of a public holiday (i) they would have been unlikely to have been high quality troops and (ii) they would have been guarding against trouble from Jesus' supporters not preventing an escaping 'corpse'. The guards probably stopped paying much attention when Jesus' supporters didnt turn up to cause trouble

          1.4.7.1.1.1. Con:
          Surely the guards would have at least been alert enough to notice a massive stone being rolled away not far from where they were standing.

            1.4.7.1.1.1.1. Con:
            Being alerted by something and actually caring are two different things.

              1.4.7.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
              It was the guards' job to notice someone trying to sneak into the tomb, so it is unlikely they wouldn't have cared about what would obviously have been an attempt to break into the tomb.

            1.4.7.1.1.1.2. Con:
            Jusus leaving the tomb (having survived the crucifixion) and the stone being rolled away don't have to have happened at the same time. More plausible explaination. 1. Jesus recovers and quietly squeezes out from behind the stone without alerting the guards e.g. around dawn. 2. Other people (perhaps responding to rumours Jesus had been seen alive) roll away the stone to look inside 3. The body is indeed missing

              1.4.7.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
              It is unlikely Jesus would have been able to "squeeze out from behind the stone" because the stone was probably placed specifically to seal the tomb, which means that a gap large enough for Jesus would have been unlikely.

        1.4.7.1.2. Con:
        In Luke 24:3 [393] we read that the women were confused about why there was no body in Jesus's tomb. If there were guards there, they could have asked the guards to clear up their confusion.

          1.4.7.1.2.1. Con:
          It is possible that the guards didn't know what happened either, so the women remained confused after asking the guards.

          1.4.7.1.2.2. Con:
          The angels could have appeared before the worked up the courage to ask the guards.

        1.4.7.1.3. Con:
        The guard story assumes that Jesus predicted his resurrection and that the Jews but not the disciples understood this prediction, which is questionable.

          1.4.7.1.3.1. Con:
          The Gospels describe Jesus predicting his resurrection.

            1.4.7.1.3.1.1. Pro:
            Jesus predicts his resurrection in Mark 8:31 [394].

            1.4.7.1.3.1.2. Pro:
            Jesus predicts his resurrection in John 2:19 [395].

          1.4.7.1.3.2. Pro:
          -> See 1.3.6.1.5.1.4.1.

          1.4.7.1.3.3. Pro:
          In Matthew 27:63 [396] within the guard story, the Jews understand Jesus's resurrection prediction.

          1.4.7.1.3.4. Con:
          William Lane Craig writes [397], "It is possible that the actions of the Jews were not motivated by any knowledge of resurrection prophecies at all, but were simply an afterthought to prevent any possible trouble that could be caused at the tomb by the disciples during the feast."

        1.4.7.1.4. Pro:
        Gospel of Peter 31 [398] supports the guard story.

          1.4.7.1.4.1. Con:
          This non-canonical gospel includes incredible claims about the resurrection that are not in any canonical gospel and defy all historical credibility, like massive figures emerging from the tom [399]b and a talking cross [400]. Thus, it is highly suspect and cannot bear any probative weight.

        1.4.7.1.5. Con:
        The guard story is probably fake because it involves the risen Jesus appearing to pagans.

          1.4.7.1.5.1. Con:
          Jesus did appear to unbelievers elsewhere in the Bible.

            1.4.7.1.5.1.1. Pro:
            Jesus appears to the unbelieving Saul in Acts 9 [401].

            1.4.7.1.5.1.2. Pro:
            Jesus appears to the unbelieving Thomas in John 20:24-29 [402].

          1.4.7.1.5.2. Con:
          The guards may never have seen the risen Jesus. Matthew 28:6 [403] says, "He is not here; he has risen, just as he said ..." implying that Jesus has already left. (reasonablefaith.org [404])

        1.4.7.1.6. Con:
        "The chief priests and Pharisees would not go to Pilate on the Sabbath day" (reasonablefaith.org [405]), which makes the guard story less plausible.

          1.4.7.1.6.1. Con:
          The Pharisees were hypocrites and may not have followed their own rules in this case (Matthew 15:7-9 [406]).

        1.4.7.1.7. Pro:
        The guard story was probably not made up by early Christians to refute the stolen body theory.

          1.4.7.1.7.1. Pro:
          The story lacks details that would have probably been included in an account designed specifically to refute the stolen body theory.

            1.4.7.1.7.1.1. Con:
            The account explicitly refers to the stolen body theory (Matt 27:64, 28:13-15), bringing attention to it in an obvious attempt to preclude it and denounce it. These are exactly the details we'd expect to see if the story were an apologetic designed to refute the theory.

              1.4.7.1.7.1.1.1. Con:
              This implies that both pro-resurrection thinkers and anti-resurrection thinkers, would have been aware of the doctrine of resurrection. So even if this is indicative of designed refutation, someone had to raise the objection, proving that Jesus did get crucified, was buried, and was claimed to have risen on the 3rd day.

                1.4.7.1.7.1.1.1.1. Con:
                If they had already stole the body, and now were being accused of it, this "stolen body" accusation would have been real at the time the gospels were written.

                  1.4.7.1.7.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                  There hasn't been any hard evidence for the body theft, the intended deception, and no record of dissent from people who had said "Jesus's body was stolen."

            1.4.7.1.7.1.2. Pro:
            Matthew never explicitly says that the guards checked the tomb at the beginning of their post, which would probably have been added if the story was made up just to refute the stolen body theory. (reasonablefaith.org [407])

            1.4.7.1.7.1.3. Pro:
            William Lane Craig writes [408], "...if the story is an apologetic fiction designed to preclude the theft of the body by the disciples, then the story is not entirely successful, for there is an obvious time period during which the disciples could have stolen the body undetected, namely between six o'clock Friday night and sometime Saturday morning."

              1.4.7.1.7.1.3.1. Con:
              Jews are forbidden to do any work, including carrying, on the sabbath, which begins at dusk on Friday. As Jesus' disciples were all religious Jews, they couldn't possibly have carried off the body during this time period.

                1.4.7.1.7.1.3.1.1. Con:
                That a person is "forbidden" to do something is not proof that they did not do something.

        1.4.7.1.8. Pro:
        If the guard story was false, it would have been easy for the Jews to check that it was false. (reasonablefaith.org [409])

          1.4.7.1.8.1. Con:
          By the time Matthew was written (about 80 CE) [410], Jerusalem was utterly destroyed and the Jews banished from it, so there would be no possible way to check it other than by memory.

            1.4.7.1.8.1.1. Pro:
            There is no way to check such an event 50+ years after it allegedly happened.

          1.4.7.1.8.2. Pro:
          The fact that "the Jewish/ Christian controversy no doubt originated in Jerusalem" would have made it very easy for the Jews to refute the guard story, assuming it were false. (reasonablefaith.org [411])

            1.4.7.1.8.2.1. Con:
            Any controversy over the account of the gospel of Matthew couldn't have arisen in Jerusalem, as by the time the gospel of Matthew was circulating (post 80 CE), Jerusalem was destroyed and Jews were exiled from it.

        1.4.7.1.9. Pro:
        Matthew 27:62-66 [412] says that the tomb was guarded.

        1.4.7.1.10. Con:
        The guards probably would not have "gone to the Jewish authorities (as Matthew 28:11-15 reports) instead of the Roman governor, Pilate, to whom they were responsible. [413]" This makes the story of the guards less credible.

          1.4.7.1.10.1. Con:
          It is not a given that the guards were Roman rather than Jewish.

            1.4.7.1.10.1.1. Con:
            According to the gospel, the guards were requested from Pilate by the priests and Pharisees (Matt 27:62-64). Pilate had no authority over Jewish guards but rather only over Roman guards, whereas the priests and pharisees would not need to ask a Roman to station their own people there. Thus the guards would have been Romans, not Jews.

        1.4.7.1.11. Con:
        The story of the guards being bribed (Matthew 28:13 [414]) is unlikely, reducing the credibility of the account of the guards.

          1.4.7.1.11.1. Pro:
          The thing that the Jewish leaders expected the guards to say, "His disciples came during the night and stole him away while we were asleep [415]," is a weak cover story because the guards would not have known that the body was stolen specifically by the disciples if the guards were sleeping at the time. The priests would have been smarter than to offer this cover story [416].

            1.4.7.1.11.1.1. Con:
            The priests may not have been able to think of a better cover story.

            1.4.7.1.11.1.2. Con:
            The leaders probably included the disciples specifically in their cover story because "who else would steal the body?" (reasonablefaith.org [417])

          1.4.7.1.11.2. Pro:
          The punishment for Roman guards sleeping on the job is so severe that "if they admitted their sleep they were as good as pronouncing their own death sentences [418]." Thus, it is unlikely that the guards could have been bribed to say that they were sleeping.

            1.4.7.1.11.2.1. Con:
            The guards were probably Jewish, not Roman.

              1.4.7.1.11.2.1.1. Pro:
              In Matthew 28:11 [419] the guards report to the chief priests, not the Romans.

              1.4.7.1.11.2.1.2. Con:
              In Gospel of Peter 43-45 [420] the guards report to Pilate, which would imply they are Roman.

                1.4.7.1.11.2.1.2.1. Con:
                William Lane Craig [421] writes, "this is probably read into the tradition and may be designed to emphasize the strength of the guard."

            1.4.7.1.11.2.2. Con:
            William Lane Craig writes [422], "perhaps the Jews' promise to 'satisfy the governor' meant telling him the truth about the guards' loyal service, if they would agree to lie to the people."

        1.4.7.1.12. Con:
        The guard story is probably a later legend.

          1.4.7.1.12.1. Pro:
          There is no reason to set a guard over a corpse: this was not done for dead bodies as a rule.

          1.4.7.1.12.2. Pro:
          If the guard story was not a later legend, the story would have been found in at least one of Mark, Luke, and John, not just Matthew.

            1.4.7.1.12.2.1. Con:
            Over a chapter's worth of material in Mark is missing from Luke [423], so the fact that a story is missing from one or more of the gospels is no reason to deny the story (reasonablefaith.org [424]).

            1.4.7.1.12.2.2. Con:
            The gospel writers had different motivations and perspectives, so there is no reason to believe that each of the writers included all important stories of Jesus's life.

          1.4.7.1.12.3. Pro:
          Matthew 28:15 [425] says, "So the soldiers took the money and did as they were instructed. And this story has been widely circulated among the Jews to this very day." This sounds like a significant period of time has elapsed between the alleged guard story and when Matthew was written, allowing for a legend to develop.

            1.4.7.1.12.3.1. Con:
            "To this very day" does not have to imply that much time. One could easily say (as of January 2021) that "to this very day the pandemic continues," but only about a year of the pandemic has passed.

      1.4.7.2. Con:
      It is not known or not known for sure whether the guards were never distracted or bribed and whether the guarded entry was the only possible entry to the tomb.

    1.4.8. Con:
    The Romans crucifying Jesus would have checked that he was dead before they left.

      1.4.8.1. Pro:
      Retief and Cilliers 2003 [426] states, "The attending Roman guards could only leave the site after the victim had died, and were known to precipitate death by means of deliberate fracturing of the tibia and/or fibula, spear stab wounds into the heart, sharp blows to the front of the chest, or a smoking fire built at the foot of the cross to asphyxiate the victim."

      1.4.8.2. Con:
      The Romans did not break Jesus' bones (John 19:36) and there is no gospel suggestion of 'sharp blows to the front of the chest' or 'smoking fires'. See:The history and pathology of crucifixion [427] F P Retief [428] 1 [429], L Cilliers [430], 2003

      1.4.8.3. Con:
      They could have checked but been wrong

        1.4.8.3.1. Con:
        If they were expected to check if the victim was dead before leaving, it was probably also the case that they were trained how to make sure the victim was dead. Otherwise, they wouldn't be able to carry out their orders.

        1.4.8.3.2. Pro:
        As stated elsewhere you have a great faith in squaddies. The soldiers would have been simple people doing a routine low grade thankless job just before a public holiday. They were not medical experts with modern equipment

    1.4.9. Con:
    A Jesus that had nearly died from crucifixion and was severely injured probably would not have been strong and healthy-looking enough to inspire such intense faith in the disciples he met (e.g. John 20:27-28 [431]).

      1.4.9.1. Con:
      A dead man walking would get people's attention, especially if he had said he was going to return from the dead (Mark 8:31 [432], Luke 9:22 [433]),

        1.4.9.1.1. Con:
        If Jesus was walking around critically injured, Thomas probably would have said, "Are you okay?", not "My Lord and My God!"

    1.4.10. Pro:
    When Jesus first emerged from the tomb he expected something supernatural was about to occur beyond his mere physical survival. John 20 17 says: Jesus said, “Do not hold on to me, for I have not yet ascended to the Father.’” This is consistent with the idea that Jesus had physically suvived the crucifixion and that he believed that something supernatural was about to occur but had not happened yet

      1.4.10.1. Con:
      That "something supernatural" was his ascension.

    1.4.11. Con:
    If Jesus survived the crucifixion, he would have claimed to have been resuscitated, not resurrected [434].

      1.4.11.1. Con:
      Not if he was expecting an 'end-of-days' event and was already expecting God to raise people from the dead

        1.4.11.1.1. Con:
        Jesus would have immediately realized it was not the 'end-of-days' once he failed to see everyone else climbing out of their graves (Daniel 12:2 [435]) just like he did.

      1.4.11.2. Con:
      That assumes proof of the bible's accuracy. Proof of the bible's accuracy cannot be assumed in a debate regarding the bible's accuracy

      1.4.11.3. Pro:
      If Jesus really claimed to be resuscitated, not resurrected, the early Christians wouldn't have universally come to the conclusion that he was actually resurrected.

        1.4.11.3.1. Pro:
        Ignatius claims that Jesus was resurrected in Epistle to the Smyrnaeans 3 [436].

      1.4.11.4. Con:
      This assumes integrity of Jesus' character. Since the validity of the bible cannot be assumed in this debate, the point is unsupported

    1.4.12. Con:
    A study called "The Physical Death of Jesus Christ: The 'Swoon Theory' and the Medical Response [437]" showed that medically this is very unlikely to have happened.

      1.4.12.1. Con:
      Unlikely but not impossible. And in any event, more likely than a unique divine intervention to produce the resurrection of a dead body

    1.4.13. Con:
    The appearance of Jesus Christ's mortal body would not have frightened the apostles in the way his resurrected body had frightened them (Luke 24:36-42 [438]).

    1.4.14. Con:
    An account by Titus Flavius Josephus [439] describes how out of three crucifixion victims that were taken down early and having the "greatest care" given to them, "two of them died under the physician’s hands, while the third recovered." If only 1 of 3 partial crucifixion victims who received a great amount of care survived, it is highly unlikely that a crucifixion victim who was fully crucified and placed in a tomb would survive.

      1.4.14.1. Con:
      So survival was a recognised possibility

    1.4.15. Con:
    The beating Jesus suffered was probably severe enough to "leave victims with their bones and entrails exposed [440]," making survival even more unlikely.

      1.4.15.1. Con:
      Remarkable examples of human survival are known to occur. However unlikely Jesus' survival may have been, it only had to happen once for Christianity to be founded by a group of people who genuinely believed they had seen the 'risen lord'

    1.4.16. Con:
    -> See 1.1.4.4.1.

    1.4.17. Pro:
    According to Mark 15:44 [441] Jesus "died" on the cross surprisingly quickly. This supports the idea that he was taken down prematurely, before he actually died.

      1.4.17.1. Con:
      He probably died relatively quickly because he was "scouraged nearly to death beforehand [442] [sic]."

    1.4.18. Con:
    With what we know about biology and medicine, according to the story told in the bible regarding Jesus's side being pierced [443], we can safely conclude that Jesus was dead.

    1.4.19. Con:
    The gospels report that Jesus died on the cross.

      1.4.19.1. Con:
      The gospel writers could have been mistaken

      1.4.19.2. Pro:
      Luke 23:46 [444] says, "Then Jesus, calling out with a loud voice, said, “Father, into your hands I commit my spirit!” And having said this he breathed his last."

      1.4.19.3. Pro:
      Mark 15:37 [445] says, "And Jesus uttered a loud cry and breathed his last."

    1.4.20. Con:
    Jesus, especially in a weakened state, would have been unable to move away the stone in front of his tomb so he could escape. (josh.org [446])

      1.4.20.1. Con:
      He would have had lots of motivation to find a way out before the authorities came back and found him alive

      1.4.20.2. Pro:
      The rock would have been specifically designed to prevent people from moving or squeezing past the rock to steal the body.

      1.4.20.3. Pro:
      The stone in front of Jesus's tomb would have been impossible for a single person to move.

        1.4.20.3.1. Pro:
        The stone was probably square, making it much harder to move away than a round stone.

          1.4.20.3.1.1. Pro:
          According to archaeologist Amos Kloner [447], "more than 98 percent of the Jewish tombs from this period, called the Second Temple period (c. first century B.C.E. to 70 C.E.), were closed with square blocking stones."

          1.4.20.3.1.2. Con:
          The stone is described as being "rolled" into place (Matthew 27:60 [448]), which means it must have been round.

        1.4.20.3.2. Pro:
        The stone is described as "megas [449]," or "great," which probably means it was very heavy.

    1.4.21. Pro:
    He simply survived the crucifixion, regained consiousness sometime on the Saturday night, folded the grave clothes whilst wondering what to do next and then left before the authorities came back and caught him

  1.5. Con:
  The resurrection was not physical, but rather spiritual.

    1.5.1. Pro:
    The apostle Paul, who claims to have seen the risen Jesus [450], says that what he saw was spiritual, not a flesh and blood [451] man [452].

      1.5.1.1. Con:
      That Paul described the changing of the perishable into the unperishable [453] doesn't disprove the physical resurrection. Instead what is implied is a changing of the quality of the physical body.

        1.5.1.1.1. Con:
        While it may not "disprove" a corporeal resurrection, it does strongly suggest that there wasn't one. It is some evidence against, whereas the evidence for is much weaker.

        1.5.1.1.2. Con:
        The "changing of the quality", according to Paul, is not what resurrection consists in: the soul is "reclothed" in a glorious spiritual body. That new body is not merely an updated form of the old, but rather an entirely new body, wholly distinct from the old one.

        1.5.1.1.3. Con:
        What Paul described is not a modification or change in quality of the physical body. Rather, it is the replacement of the body with a wholly new and different body.

      1.5.1.2. Con:
      Paul's conversion and thereby his interaction with Jesus, was some time [454] after the ascension of Jesus into heaven. So if Jesus were still interacting with terrestrial humans, it is reasonable that it would be in some ethereal form, lacking physical substance. This doesn't not imply that Jesus only had a spiritual form after his death.

        1.5.1.2.1. Con:
        Paul's epistles are the earliest Christian source in existence, predating Acts by up to 100 years. Paul's firsthand account gives no indication whatsoever that there was any belief in Christ's resurrection being corporeal. Paul implies that his vision of the risen Christ was not fundamentally different than the vision experienced by any other apostle.

      1.5.1.3. Pro:
      From Paul's firsthand testimony, we can infer that the corporeal resurrections mentioned in Luke-Acts and John are later apologetic developments, included as straightforward corroboration for the claim of the resurrection of Jesus.

    1.5.2. Pro:
    Exactly what this "resurrection" means--whether this is a corporeal resurrected body like a resuscitated corpse or a spiritual resurrection without a corporeal body--is not consistent within the corpus of the NT.

      1.5.2.1. Pro:
      The gospels of Luke and John are the only accounts of the resurrected Jesus with an unambiguously corporeal body, capable of eating food, and being handled.

      1.5.2.2. Con:
      Resurrection was understood to mean, Alive again. At no point is the word used to describe an immaterial event.

      1.5.2.3. Con:
      Mark 6:14, while trying to discern who Jesus was, Mark records people stating that Jesus was John the Baptizer, "risen from the dead." With Jesus physically standing before them it would be unreasonable for them to have confused physical Jesus for an apparition.

    1.5.3. Pro:
    The gospel of Mark does not describe the resurrection as physical: the risen body of Jesus is never mentioned. Rather, following Paul, Mark implies that the resurrection was spiritual.

      1.5.3.1. Con:
      According to Mark [455], Jesus body wasn't present, which means that if resurrection was only spiritual, it still has a profound impact on the physical body that remains. Such that the body was missing.

        1.5.3.1.1. Con:
        The disappearance of the body is a trope in Mediterranean apotheosis narratives [456] in the centuries before Christ. It does not imply that the resurrection was corporeal but rather that the person has been divinized, taken up to heaven.

      1.5.3.2. Con:
      According to Mark [457], Jesus had "Risen [458]" which outside of the resurrection of Jesus, was also used to describe the event of Lazarus being "risen."

        1.5.3.2.1. Pro:
        Lazarus had to be unbound from his burial wraps, which implies that his physical body was reanimated and in need of unbinding. This same kind of physical "risen" can be implied by ever other account of "risen" in the new testament.

        1.5.3.2.2. Con:
        The story of the resurrection of Lazarus occurs only in the gospel of John, which was written around 30 years after the gospel of Mark.

        1.5.3.2.3. Con:
        The Greek verb used in both cases is ἐγείρω, which is a generic verb with a breadth of meanings [459]: "to awaken, to raise, cause to appear, incite", used over 140 times in the NT, and often is used of those who are alive when they get up or wake up. It is not a technical word with a narrow technical meaning, and so that it it used of both Lazarus and Jesus cannot itself show that their state after death was the same.

          1.5.3.2.3.1. Con:
          To ἐγείρω from sleep is an implicit call to one's awakened state. If resurrection were only spiritual, then we'd expect to see ἐγείρω used to describe sleep walking.

      1.5.3.3. Pro:
      Paul says in 1 Corinthians 15:50 [460], "...flesh and blood cannot inherit the kingdom of God..." This implies that Paul believes the resurrection is spiritual, because otherwise the physical resurrection body would inherit the kingdom of God.

        1.5.3.3.1. Pro:
        Paul implies the resurrection is not physical. [461]

          1.5.3.3.1.1. Pro:
          Jesus could have appeared to the disciples in spirit form, not as a physical body.

            1.5.3.3.1.1.1. Con:
            In Luke 24:39 [462] Jesus says, "Look at my hands and my feet. It is I myself! Touch me and see; a ghost does not have flesh and bones, as you see I have." He thus denies that he is a spirit being without a physical body.

              1.5.3.3.1.1.1.1. Con:
              Jesus could have claimed to have a physical body without actually having a physical body after his resurrection.

                1.5.3.3.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                This would be inconsistent with his honesty and good character as reported elsewhere.

                  1.5.3.3.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                  His purported honesty and good character are only reported from one unreliable, biased text; so, we cannot automatically conclude Jesus was honest.

                    1.5.3.3.1.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                    The Gospels should be counted as four texts.

                1.5.3.3.1.1.1.1.2. Con:
                The implication in Luke 24:39 is that the disciple actually did touch Jesus, thus proving Jesus's words.

              1.5.3.3.1.1.1.2. Con:
              This is a later development (Luke-Acts is typically dated to the 90s, but may be completed as late as the mid-second century) in the legend that does not reflect the original historical realities of the time shortly after the crucifixion.

                1.5.3.3.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
                The time shortly after the resurrection is detailed in Acts, which details miracles and feats of supreme courage to tell the good news of Jesus defeating death.

            1.5.3.3.1.1.2. Pro:
            The earliest writings, including Paul's epistles, do not mention Jesus physically appearing to anyone. (jashow.org [463])

              1.5.3.3.1.1.2.1. Con:
              Mark implies that Jesus would physically appear to people. (jashow.org [464])

                1.5.3.3.1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
                In Mark 16:7 [465] the man at the tomb says, "But go, tell his disciples and Peter, 'He is going ahead of you into Galilee. There you will see him, just as he told you.'" If only a spiritual appearance of Jesus was expected, it's not clear why the man expected them to go to a specific place to see Jesus.

              1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2. Con:
              The "soma" word group is used to refer to the post-resurrection bodies of Jesus and believers, and this group is consistently used to mean "physical body."

                1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.1. Con:
                That the resurrected person has a body does not imply that this is a physical body. Paul unambiguously distinguishes the body buried ("sown") from the body raised [466] as two distinct and very different things. Thus, "body" (soma) need not imply a physical body.

                1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.2. Pro:
                The word for "body," soma, is understood to be a physical body when Paul uses it elsewhere. [467]

                1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3. Pro:
                The "soma" word group is used to refer to the post-resurrection bodies of Jesus and believers.

                  1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.1. Pro:
                  Philippians 3:20-21 [468] says, "...And we eagerly await a Savior from there, the Lord Jesus Christ, who, by the power that enables him to bring everything under his control, will transform our lowly bodies so that they will be like his glorious body."

                  1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.2. Pro:
                  In Colossians 2:9 [469], Paul describes Jesus's post-resurrection state as dwelling bodily (somatikos [470]).

                  1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.3. Pro:
                  1 Corinthians 15:37 [471], 38 [472], and 44 [473] all refer to the post-resurrection body as a "soma."

                    1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.3.1. Con:
                    Paul goes on to state explicitly in 1 Cor 15:44 [474] that the body raised is "πνευματικόν", that is, spiritual, as opposed to the buried body which is "ψυχικόν", that is, natural or animal.

                      1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.3.1.1. Con:
                      This angle dismisses any reading that might involve hyperbole, metaphors, or euphemisms. Case and point, 1 Cor 15:39 makes a distinction between Humans, Animals, Birds & Fish (as though they were different than animals,) all having different "flesh." This can be rationalized without contradiction if we consider that Paul is using the word "flesh," as a metaphor and then applying a metaphorical filter to the surrounding passage.

                        1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.3.1.1.1. Pro:
                        A metaphorical reading with consideration to 1 Cor 15 facilitate a better understanding, without cherry picking, that Paul is using some artistic license to describe a phenomenon that seems counter intuitive, (that the dead can be made alive again.)

                        1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.3.1.1.2. Con:
                        Different kinds of flesh among different animals is not and cannot be a metaphor for anything. Given that it is literally true, however, it should be interpreted as being meant literally.

                          1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.3.1.1.2.1. Con:
                          Controlling the interpretation of scripture leads to things like Mormonism, westborro baptist, crusades, papal kingdoms and all other manner of religious oppression. It is possible to interpret flesh as having been purposely generalized for the sake of illustration. Culminating in your flesh, (sinful nature), will not inherit the kingdom of God. Instead its your spirit (Godly devotion), that enters into the kingdom of God.

                          1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.3.1.1.2.2. Con:
                          Using the different types of flesh for the different types of animals is metaphor for the different body types for the natural body and the spiritual body. There is nothing significant about the use of these animals outside the illustration given, that the resurrection body will be different.

                  1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.4. Pro:
                  Romans 8:11 [475] implies that the resurrection involves giving life to a mortal body (soma).

                    1.5.3.3.1.1.2.2.3.4.1. Con:
                    This verse is referring to the effect of the Holy Spirit on people who haven't yet died.

              1.5.3.3.1.1.2.3. Con:
              Belief in a mere spiritual resurrection of Jesus would not have been enough to elicit the kinds of claims early Christians were making (p. 95 [476]).

              1.5.3.3.1.1.2.4. Pro:
              Speaking of the resurrection, Paul says "It is sown a natural body; it is raised a spiritual body. If there is a natural body, there is also a spiritual body" (1 Corinthians 15:44 [477]). Therefore the resurrected body is spiritual and not physical according to Paul.

                1.5.3.3.1.1.2.4.1. Con:
                Paul is using "natural" and "spiritual" to describe the orientation of the body toward God, not whether the body is physical. In 1 Corinthians 2:14-15 [478] the "spiritual man" is called "spiritual" not because he lacks physical form, but because he is godly.

                  1.5.3.3.1.1.2.4.1.1. Con:
                  Paul describes the resurrection body as different from the corpse as a plant is from the seed it grows from, and distinct from the "sown" corpse. [479] The dead body is perishable, of dust, weak, dishonorable; the raised body is imperishable, of heaven, powerful, glorious.

              1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5. Con:
              In Acts 13:32-34 [480] Paul expresses his belief in the physical resurrection of Jesus: "...God raised him from the dead so that he will never be subject to decay..."

                1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1. Con:
                As Paul says in 1 Cor 15, the mortal body is made of dust and is subject to decay, but the resurrection body is heavenly, imperishable, and spiritual, and distinct from the body that is sown (i.e. the corpse). Thus, assuming Paul is consistent with himself in his letters and in Acts, this implies a non-physical (non-ψυχικος) resurrection.

                  1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1. Con:
                  Paul also says in that same verse "For this perishable body must put on the imperishable, and this mortal body must put on immortality." "put on," is the Greek word ἐνδύω which is the same word that would have been used to put on a jacket.

                    1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1. Con:
                    The point Paul is making by "putting on" the new spiritual body, given that the physical body is subject to corruption and decay, is that the naked soul will "put on" this new non-physical body and the physical body will be destroyed, leaving only the new spiritual body. Like putting on a coat and taking off the shirt underneath it.

                      1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                      In 2 Cor 5:6-8 [481], we read:"...while we are at home in the body we are away from the Lord..." And "...we would rather be away from the body and at home with the Lord..." So we have to leave the body to be at home with the Lord, which we will be at the resurrection. So the body is left behind at the resurrection.

                        1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                        Not so, 1 Cor 15:50-52 states that not all will "sleep" before the coming of the lord, but all will be transformed. Therefore, when taken with the verses prior 2 Cor 5:1-5, the dwelling of the lord while different from the earthly domain, doesn't require leaving the body.

                          1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                          The transformation involves a complete change of body from a physical body, which is left behind, to a spiritual body, which is caught up and taken into the air. [482]

                            1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                            This is an appeal to ignorance fallacy as the claim provides no means or methods by which it comes to it's conclusion. Only that no positive evidence can be shown to dismiss it's own assertion.

                              1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                              The claim referred to isn't a claim based on ignorance, but on information that can be derived from scripture.

                            1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
                            2 Cor 5 [483] "For we know that if the earthly tent we live in is destroyed, we have a building from God, an eternal house in heaven...we ...long to be clothed instead with our heavenly ... For while we are in this tent, we...wish... to be clothed instead with our heavenly dwelling, so that what is mortal may be swallowed up by life." Thus, we wish to leave this "earthly tent", which will be destroyed, and "be clothed instead with our heavenly dwelling", which is our spiritual bodies.

                            1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.1.1.1.3. Pro:
                            In 2 Cor 5 [484]: "Therefore we ... know that as long as we are at home in the body we are away from the Lord... We are confident, I say, and would prefer to be away from the body and at home with the Lord." Thus, "as long as we are at home in the body we are away from the Lord" and we "would prefer to be away from the body and at home with the Lord." So being in the body is being away from the Lord, and being at home with the Lord (at the resurrection) is being away from the body.

                      1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.2. Pro:
                      In 1 Cor 15 [485]:36-38, we read: "36 What you sow does not come to life unless it dies. 37 When you sow, you do not plant the body that will be, but just a seed, perhaps of wheat or of something else. 38 But God gives it a body as he has determined, and to each kind of seed he gives its own body." Thus, the resurrection body is not the body that is "sown" which is like a seed compared to the resurrection body.

                        1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
                        Seeds have physical bodies, as do the plants that grow from them. While the body that is sown might be different than a resurrection body, this doesn't equate to an ethereal non-physical body.

                          1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
                          Paul goes on to say [486]: "44 it is sown a natural body, it is raised a spiritual body. If there is a natural body, there is also a spiritual body." So the body that is raised is imperishable, glorious, powerful, a spiritual body, utterly different than the down body. Ultimately, the plant metaphor is only a metaphor and not to be taken as a perfect analogy.

                            1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
                            Similarly, the phrases Paul uses are metaphorical, as the words "Flesh," and "Natural," can be taken as meaning, sinfulness of the heart, and the words "Spirit," and "imperishable," can be taken as meaning, Godly of heart.

                              1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.2.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                              Paul uses Flesh and Spirit as means to distinguish behaviors in his letter to Galatia [487].

                                1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.2.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                                In this passage, Paul is clearly referring to behavior while still in the mortal fleshly body, not to the nature of the resurrection body.

                              1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.2.1.1.1.2. Con:
                              Words CAN be taken in several ways. But they SHOULD be taken in the way that best seeks to understand the intention of the author, St. Paul. The author makes clear that he does not mean the resurrection body is only metaphorically for ethical behavior, "Godly of heart".

                                1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.2.1.1.1.2.1. Pro:
                                In Galatians 5 [488], Paul is not discussing the resurrection body. Rather, he is referring to mortal life, when there is both spirit and mortal flesh. But St. Paul is explicit that flesh will not be in the resurrection [489] body.

                                  1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.2.1.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
                                  Paul's use or disuse of metaphorical/analogous language is under no obligation to restrict such use or disuse to one subject.

                                1.5.3.3.1.1.2.5.1.1.1.2.1.1.1.2.2. Con:
                                The author's intent can be summed up that he trying to describe the resurrection/resurrection body. What is being contested is that author is implying either a non-physical resurrection or a physical resurrection. Concluding that it is clear that the author affirms one side over the other is to ignore the entire branches discourse on the issue.

              1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6. Con:
              Paul's use of "raised" in 1 Corinthians 15:3-5 [490] implies a physical resurrection.

                1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1. Con:
                Paul uses "raised" in other verses to refer to a spiritual resurrection.

                  1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.1. Pro:
                  In 1 Cor 15:42,44, Paul says: "So will it be with the resurrection of the dead. The body that is sown is perishable, it is raised imperishable;...it is sown a natural body, it is raised a spiritual body. If there is a natural body, there is also a spiritual body."

                  1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.2. Pro:
                  Colossians 2:12 [491] uses "raised" to describe a spiritual resurrection. Therefore, the use of "raised" does not establish a physical resurrection.

                    1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.2.1. Con:
                    "συνεγείρω" is not the word used in 1 Corinthians 15, "ἐγείρω" and just in english, prefixes and suffixes can alter words drastically like in "conclude - finish" and "include - bring along" and "preclude - make impossible" and "seclude - away from."

                      1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.2.1.1. Con:
                      Greek often uses prefixes in place of prepositions. In this case the addition of "syn" indicates "together" as in "έγειρεν [us] together".

                        1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
                        Thayer's Greek lexicon gives: "from sun and egeiro. Definition: to raise together Thayer's Greek Lexicon STRONGS NT 4891: συνεγείρω [492] ... to raise together, to cause to rise together; in the N. T. tropically, to raise up together from moral death to a new and blessed life devoted to God."

                  1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.3. Pro:
                  Ephesians 2:1, 5-6 [493] uses "raised" to mean a spiritual resurrection.

                    1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.3.1. Con:
                    -> See 1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.2.1.

                    1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.3.2. Con:
                    This is a laymen's assessment. The word used in Ephesians 2:6 that is sometimes translated as "raised" is "συνήγειρεν - synēgeiren," which makes an argument about translations, not about resurrection.

                      1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.3.2.1. Con:
                      In any case, it does not refer to a physical resurrection, contra the great-grandparent claim, but rather to something happening in the spiritual realm.

                        1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.3.2.1.1. Con:
                        Living apart from sin happens in the physical world.

                    1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.3.3. Con:
                    This is a laymen's assessment. The word used in Ephesians 2:6 that is sometimes translated as "raised" is "συνήγειρεν - synēgeiren," which in context was talking about God's ability to empower believers to live in Christ, apart from their trespasses.

                      1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.3.3.1. Con:
                      -> See 1.5.3.3.1.1.2.6.1.3.2.1.

        1.5.3.3.2. Con:
        If the spiritual-man exists, then it is intertwined with the physical person in a way that makes it homogeneous meaning you cannot have one without the other. It follows then that resurrection must be physical and spiritual or neither.

          1.5.3.3.2.1. Con:
          Paul explicitly says the resurrected person is given a new spiritual body [494] as different from their physical body as a seed is from a fully grown plant. Thus, there is no "spiritual-man" that exists before the resurrection.

        1.5.3.3.3. Con:
        Paul when writing to the Thessalonians stated that those who, "are alive, who are left, will be caught up together with them in the clouds to meet the Lord in the air, and so we will always be with the Lord. [495]" And one cannot be alive without a physical body to be caught up with.

          1.5.3.3.3.1. Con:
          "Spiritual" [496], as Paul uses it, is a sort of non-physical substance. Not all substance is physical, as Paul sees it.

        1.5.3.3.4. Con:
        Luke describes the interaction [497] between the risen Jesus to be discernable by the physical marks on Jesus's body.

          1.5.3.3.4.1. Con:
          The author of Luke-Acts holds a view strictly irreconcilable with Paul's view. We should not assume that all the different writings in the New Testament are christologically homogeneous.

        1.5.3.3.5. Pro:
        -> See 1.5.3.3.1.1.2.4.

        1.5.3.3.6. Con:
        "Flesh" is frequently used in Scripture as a metaphor for worldliness [498] and resistance to God. If this meaning is uses here, the passage teaches that worldly things cannot inherit the kingdom of God, not that literal flesh cannot.

          1.5.3.3.6.1. Pro:
          When warning against temptation, Jesus says in Matthew 26:41 [499] and Mark 14:38 [500] "the spirit is willing, but the flesh is weak."

          1.5.3.3.6.2. Pro:
          In Romans 7:5 [501] Paul says "while we were in the flesh" to mean "before we believed."

          1.5.3.3.6.3. Pro:
          In Romans 8:1-17 [502] "flesh" and "spirit" are frequently used to refer to ungodliness and godliness respectively.

          1.5.3.3.6.4. Pro:
          2 Corinthians 10:2 [503] uses "flesh" as a metaphor for worldliness.

          1.5.3.3.6.5. Pro:
          Galatians 5:13-26 [504] uses "flesh" and "spirit" to refer to godliness and ungodliness respectively.

          1.5.3.3.6.6. Pro:
          Colossians 2:23 [505] uses "indulgence of the flesh" to mean "worldly things."

            1.5.3.3.6.6.1. Con:
            The grammatical structures [506] used in Paul's other letters does not match the grammar of Colossians. It therefore could be concluded that Paul was not the author of Colossians.

              1.5.3.3.6.6.1.1. Con:
              Stylistic differences appear [507] among the undisputed letters of 2 Corinthians, Romans, and Philippians. Thus, stylistic differences are not sufficient to reject the Pauline authorship of a letter.

                1.5.3.3.6.6.1.1.1. Pro:
                Philippians tends to use [508] "personal asides and imperatives."

                1.5.3.3.6.6.1.1.2. Pro:
                2 Corinthians tends to use [509] "a clear and simple writing style along with much irony and indirectness."

              1.5.3.3.6.6.1.2. Con:
              Every major church father [510] except Marcion either approved of Colossians or they at least made mention of it.

              1.5.3.3.6.6.1.3. Con:
              Philemon confirms the book of Colossians because both books describe Onesimus and Archippus as living in the same city, yet this is a detail a forger would have been unlikely to design. Thus, the authenticity of Philemon also supports Colossians. (eriknmanning.medium.com [511])

                1.5.3.3.6.6.1.3.1. Con:
                As the detail about the names would be easy to fabricate, it does not indicate anything about the authenticity of the letter. [512]

                1.5.3.3.6.6.1.3.2. Pro:
                According to Colossians, Onesimus and Archippus live in the same city.

                  1.5.3.3.6.6.1.3.2.1. Pro:
                  In the farewell in the book of Colossians, the author mentions Onesimus (Colossians 4:9 [513]) and Archippus (Colossians 4:17 [514]) as though both were from Colossae.

                1.5.3.3.6.6.1.3.3. Pro:
                According to Philemon, Onesimus and Archippus live in the same city.

                  1.5.3.3.6.6.1.3.3.1. Pro:
                  Philemon 1:1-2 [515] implies that Archippus lived in the same city as Philemon, yet Onesimus is Philemon's slave (Philemon 1:8-25 [516]). Thus, Onesimus and Archippus live in the same city.

              1.5.3.3.6.6.1.4. Con:
              The writing style of authors tends to change [517] as their writing technique evolves or their audience changes, so small differences between the style of Colossians and epistles like Romans and 1 Corinthians should not be used to reject the former.
