        The kind of post mortem apparitions people commonly have in mistaking that a loved one is still alive would not explain the physical presence or physical exchanges that are detailed about Jesus eating or Thomas physically touching him outside of mass hallucination event [724]. Therefore it is more likely that these testimonies were not only earnest, but grounded in a sober minded reality.

          1.8.1.3.2.1. Con:
          Alternatively, the descriptions of the appearances in the NT are not accurate to what actually happened, and what actually happened involved no physicality. That is, the NT accounts may be fabricated or embellished.

            1.8.1.3.2.1.1. Con:
            This parent claim [725] may have been written with embellishment and fabrications. This is an "anti-christ-of-the-gaps" argument, in which, if a possibility exists then it must mean that documentation is not 100% and therefore unreliable.

      1.8.1.4. Con:
      Jesus gives no new teachings after dying, despite spending 40 days with the disciples. Before dying, Jesus gave very many teachings, thus, the total absence of postmortem teachings that can be reliably sourced to the historical Jesus is extremely suspicious.

        1.8.1.4.1. Pro:
        If my friend came back from the dead, I'd ask him "what was it like to die? Where did you go? What happens after we die?" But the gospels don't show any interest in such questions.

          1.8.1.4.1.1. Con:
          Jesus had already answered these questions while preaching about the afterlife before his death.

            1.8.1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
            In Matthew 25:31-46 [726] uses a parable to describe the differing fates of the saved and unsaved in the afterlife.

              1.8.1.4.1.1.1.1. Con:
              This doesn't describe what anyone will experience after death except in extremely minimal, abstract, and metaphorical senses.

            1.8.1.4.1.1.2. Pro:
            In John 3:16 [727] Jesus talks about how in the afterlife, believers will have eternal life.

              1.8.1.4.1.1.2.1. Con:
              Jews already believed in a general resurrection so this offers no new information.

            1.8.1.4.1.1.3. Pro:
            In John 14:1-4 [728] Jesus describes the afterlife.

              1.8.1.4.1.1.3.1. Con:
              This is an extremely vague and informative description of heaven, not a description of what one can expect to experience after death.

          1.8.1.4.1.2. Con:
          The gospels don't mention this because the more important message in the opinion of the gospel authors was the resurrection itself. Thus, the resurrection itself was emphasized.

        1.8.1.4.2. Con:
        After Jesus's resurrection, John 21:25 [729] says, "Jesus did many other things as well. If every one of them were written down, I suppose that even the whole world would not have room for the books that would be written."

          1.8.1.4.2.1. Con:
          Rather than actually describe any of the things Jesus did, the author only says that he did so many things it is beyond reckoning. This seems to be an obvious dodge.

            1.8.1.4.2.1.1. Pro:
            John could have given three or four examples and then said "there are very many more". Instead, he gives none.

              1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1. Con:
              The miraculous catch in John 21:1-9 [730] should count as an example.

                1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1. Con:
                This doesn't appear in any of the other gospels.

                  1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                  If we have multiple accounts we actually count on differing stories. If everyone tells the same exact story, that would imply some kind of set up, since we know that eyewitnesses naturally suffer from bias [731] and selective recollection [732]

                    1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                    Witnesses can be expected to agree on major details but differ on minor details. This is a major detail and thus we would expect the witnesses to agree about it.

                      1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                      Major and minor details do not have any bearing on witness bias or selective recollection if those giving an account were not witness to some specific event.

                    1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2. Con:
                    Two witnesses of a genuine miracle will both agree they witnessed a miracle.

                      1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
                      John 21:1-9 never claims that Matthew, Mark, or Luke witnessed the miraculous catch.

                        1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
                        It does claim Peter witnessed it. Peter was a primary figure in the early Church and, according to Church tradition, served as one of the main sources for the gospels.

                          1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
                          Traditionally, the author of the gospel of Mark is identified with John Mark [733], the traveling companion of Peter.

                          1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2. Con:
                          There is no guarantee that Peter remembered every encounter he had with Jesus.

                            1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1. Con:
                            The event described is literally a miracle, not an everyday event. There is good reason to think Peter would remember every miracle Jesus performed.

                              1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
                              This is especially so because it was one of the last ones Jesus allegedly performed, and we remember what happened most recently better than what happened earlier on.

                                1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
                                John 21:25 [734] implies there were many miracles after the miraculous catch at the beginning of John 21.

                                  1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.1.1.1. Con:
                                  Then the fish miracle must have been remarkable to have been the only one worth mentioning explicitly.

                              1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.2. Pro:
                              This is especially so because the Jesus who performed the miracle was the risen Jesus who has died shortly before.

                              1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3. Con:
                              Jesus did such a huge number of miracles over his lifetime that it is unlikely Peter could have remembered them all.

                                1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1. Con:
                                Jesus didn't do a great number after he was resurrected. Thus, we would expect Peter to remember those ones as being easy to remember.

                                  1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1. Con:
                                  John 21:25 [735] implies that Jesus performed many miracles after his resurrection.

                                    1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1.1. Con:
                                    The fact that he only records one explicitly implies it was the only one worth recording.

                                    1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1.2. Con:
                                    Then it remains inexplicable why the other gospels record none, rather than one or more of the "many".

                                      1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1.2.1. Con:
                                      This can be explained by the intention of each author to emphasize different parts of Jesus's life.

                                        1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
                                        Completely excluding all of the "many" supernatural events that took place after Jesus' death crosses the line from threshold to irresponsible or incomplete documentation.

                                          1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
                                          If the gospels are claimed to be accurate to the events they relate, excluding all the "many" miracles after Jesus' death would amount to inaccuracy by omission.

                                            1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1.2.1.1.1.1. Con:
                                            Choosing not to narrate some events is not "inaccuracy by omission", as long as this is not done to give a misleading impression (such as only leaving out the events unfavorable to one's views).

                                          1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1.2.1.1.2. Pro:
                                          If the gospels are incomplete to this extent, it greatly undermines their general credibility: what else could they be leaving out? What other corners are they cutting?

                                            1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1.2.1.1.2.1. Con:
                                            The principle of Sola Scriptura tells us the Bible as a whole contains all the information needed to live a good Christian life. We can thus be confident we aren't missing anything we need to know.

                                              1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
                                              The doctrine of sola scriptura is merely dogma: it is taken on faith and is not based on any independent reason. One can take it on faith, but absent such faith one will need to use good reasons.

                                  1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.2. Con:
                                  John 20:30 [736] implies that Jesus performed many miracles after he was resurrected.

                                    1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.2.1. Con:
                                    The absence of any explicit reference to these "many miracles" by any NT author or other early Christian suggests this verse was included to cover the many disparate legends that arose about Jesus rather than corresponding to actual historical events.

                                      1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.2.1.1. Con:
                                      It is more likely that John was simply running out of time and/or papyrus by the time he reached the end of John 20.

                                1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.2. Pro:
                                -> See 1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.1.

                                1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.3. Pro:
                                -> See 1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.3.1.2.

                            1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.2. Pro:
                            Nor that it was the intent of the author of Mark, who we assume interview Peter, was intent on declaring every miracle ad naseum.

                              1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.2.1. Con:
                              Acts of God (miracles) are always worth relating and hearing and thus can never be done "ad nauseam."

                                1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.2.1.1. Con:
                                The author of Mark may not have shared the view that every detail about God needed to be recorded. After all, If God is God and does miracles, there isn't enough paper in the world to detail all that God has done.

                                  1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.2.1.1.1. Con:
                                  As John only records a few miracles that occurred after Jesus died, these were likely the most important ones. After all, he recorded many that happened prior to Jesus death. Thus, it would make sense for Mark and the other synoptics to record at least the one John considered worth mentioning.

                                  1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.1.2.1.1.2.2.1.1.2. Con:
                                  John only records this miracle after Jesus died. Either there were several such miracles or only one. Either way, we would expect the synoptics to record at least one such miracle after Jesus died, if there were multiple to choose from (there was at least the one mentioned in John).

                  1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
                  If some major event happened we would expect it to be the "talk of the town," let alone a miracle, which would likely be the talk of at least several towns.

                    1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
                    Jesus performed many miracles in the past. Plausibly people began getting used to these stories.

                  1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.3. Pro:
                  As the early Church was exchanging information among itself, we would expect stories and claims to spread throughout the Church, so that if one person knew something about Jesus, others would soon learn about it. If John learned about it, he must have learned it from someone who had seen it. But since the other NT writers all predate John, we'd expect them to have heard about it. As it's an important occurrence, we'd expect them to write about it. That none of them do suggests it's fabricated.

                    1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.3.1. Con:
                    Not every Gospel writer felt compelled to record every miracle they knew about.

                      1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
                      John 20:30 [737] says, "Jesus performed many other signs in the presence of his disciples, which are not recorded in this book."

                        1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.3.1.1.1. Con:
                        The miracle in question is only in the Gospel of John and not in any other part of the NT.

                  1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.1.4. Pro:
                  Peter is an active participant in the story. Yet the gospels, which allegedly use Peter as a source, and Paul, who met and traveled with Peter, don't mention the story.

                1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.2. Con:
                This example is a literary device,.referring back to similar previous verses about fishing.

                1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.3. Con:
                One example is not at all sufficient.

                1.8.1.4.2.1.1.1.4. Con:
                No significant new teachings are given in this example.

              1.8.1.4.2.1.1.2. Con:
              In John 20:19 [738] Jesus teleports inside of a locked room.

            1.8.1.4.2.1.2. Con:
            The author probably wrote this not as a "dodge", but because he was running out of room on his parchment.

              1.8.1.4.2.1.2.1. Con:
              There was more parchment available if he wanted to write more.

                1.8.1.4.2.1.2.1.1. Con:
                Parchment would have been very expensive at the time.

                  1.8.1.4.2.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
                  There was enough to write the gospels themselves, as well as copy them.

        1.8.1.4.3. Con:
        The 40 days was a small proportion of Jesus's total ministry; thus a proportionately small amount of text is used to describe it.

          1.8.1.4.3.1. Pro:
          This is the same reason not much detail is given on Jesus's 40 days in the wilderness.

            1.8.1.4.3.1.1. Con:
            Jesus was mostly alone in the wilderness not doing very much. This explains why it is mostly glossed over and not much described.

          1.8.1.4.3.2. Con:
          Jesus' final days are described in rich detail. Jesus' adolescence is ignored. The gospels do not allocate proportional time to each period of Jesus' life: they write about what mattered. Clearly, then, Jesus' time on earth as a resurrected human didn't much matter.

        1.8.1.4.4. Con:
        -> See 1.2.4.1.1.

        1.8.1.4.5. Con:
        Jesus revealed that Peter would be martyred (John 21:18-19 [739]).

          1.8.1.4.5.1. Con:
          As the gospel of John was written (around 100 CE) after Peter had already been martyred (around 67 CE), this is in no sense a prophecy.

        1.8.1.4.6. Pro:
        There are no examples of post-mortem teachings that occur in more than one gospel, even in the synoptic gospels which otherwise show significant similarities in what they narrate Jesus saying and doing pre-mortem.

      1.8.1.5. Con:
      We have no extra-biblical proof that Jesus died

        1.8.1.5.1. Con:
        Tacitus writes in Annals 15.44 [740] that Jesus was crucified.

        1.8.1.5.2. Con:
        Mara Bar-Serapion [741] refers to the execution of Jesus.

    1.8.2. Con:
    It was a dramatic account that supported what otherwise would merely be articles of faith, namely, that Jesus rose bodily from the dead on the third day.

      1.8.2.1. Pro:
      The empty tomb story is a fabrication.

        1.8.2.1.1. Pro:
        Jesus' tomb would very likely have been a pilgrimage site for early Christians. However, the location of the tomb was unknown for hundreds of years until Helen designated the location for the Church of the Holy Sepulchre. To this day, many archaeologists dispute where Jesus' tomb really was, according to the gospel narratives. The best explanation is that Jesus was not actually buried in a tomb.

          1.8.2.1.1.1. Pro:
          At the time, it was the custom of the Roman crucifixion [742] that the corpses were never decently buried - the same very likely happened to Jesus.

            1.8.2.1.1.1.1. Pro:
            The bodies [743] of crucified people were usually left to hang on the cross for a few days for deterrence before they were thrown into a pit.

            1.8.2.1.1.1.2. Pro:
            The goal of the crucifixion was total humiliation [744]. To allow a decent burial is in contrast to this.

            1.8.2.1.1.1.3. Con:
            Yehohanan [745] was a Jew who underwent Roman crucifixion in his mid-twenties, and his body was taken down and buried in a cave.

          1.8.2.1.1.2. Pro:
          Jesus was put in a mass/unmarked grave, not a tomb.

            1.8.2.1.1.2.1. Pro:
            The Jews did not bury criminals in tombs, they buried them in common graves.

              1.8.2.1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
              Mishnah Sanhedrin 6:5 [746] says, "After the executed transgressor is taken down he is buried, and they would not bury him in his ancestral burial plot. Rather, two graveyards were established for the burial of those executed by the court..."

                1.8.2.1.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
                The burial plots were for those killed by decapitation, strangulation, stoning, or burning (Mishnah Sanhedrin 6:5 [747]). Since Jesus was crucified, this passage may not apply to him.

                  1.8.2.1.1.2.1.1.1.1. Con:
                  As all are forms of punitive execution, there is every reason to believe they would all receive the same treatment.

              1.8.2.1.1.2.1.2. Con:
              The Jews may not have considered Jesus a criminal.

                1.8.2.1.1.2.1.2.1. Con:
                The Jews in Matthew 27:11-26 [748] clearly view Jesus as a criminal.

                1.8.2.1.1.2.1.2.2. Con:
                The Sanhedrin in Mark 14:64 [749] view Jesus as guilty of blasphemy. (infidels.org [750])

          1.8.2.1.1.3. Pro:
          Jesus's body was left on the ground or on the cross and eaten by animals.

            1.8.2.1.1.3.1. Pro:
            Jesus may not have been buried at all, and was left for scavengers to consume, as was standard Roman crucifixion procedure.

          1.8.2.1.1.4. Pro:
          The Romans wouldn't have let anyone bury Jesus.

            1.8.2.1.1.4.1. Pro:
            -> See 1.8.2.1.1.1.

            1.8.2.1.1.4.2. Con:
            The Romans did allow occupied cultures to follow their traditional laws [751] in times of peace.

              1.8.2.1.1.4.2.1. Pro:
              Josephus writes [752] that the Romans did not make "their subjects ... violate their national laws" (Against Apion 2:73).

            1.8.2.1.1.4.3. Con:
            The Digest [753] (a.k.a. Pandects) is a Roman law book that shows the Romans would have allowed people to bury Jesus.

              1.8.2.1.1.4.3.1. Pro:
              Digesta 48:24:1 [754] says, " The bodies of those who are condemned to death should not be refused their relatives; and the Divine Augustus, in the Tenth Book of his Life, said that this rule had been observed..."

              1.8.2.1.1.4.3.2. Pro:
              Digesta 48:24:3 [755] says, "The bodies of persons who have been punished should be given to whoever requests them for the purpose of burial."

            1.8.2.1.1.4.4. Con:
            The Jews were permitted to bury crucifixion victims before sundown.

              1.8.2.1.1.4.4.1. Con:
              This could only have happened with the permission of the authorities. It is unlikely the Roman authorities would have permitted a political insurgent like Jesus to be taken down and buried, especially one also condemned and rejected by the Jewish authorities.

              1.8.2.1.1.4.4.2. Pro:
              A quote from Josephus implies that the Jews were permitted to bury crucifixion victims before sundown.

                1.8.2.1.1.4.4.2.1. Pro:
                Josephus writes [756], "Nay, [the Idumeans] proceeded to that degree of impiety, as to cast away their dead bodies without burial, although the Jews used to take so much care of the burial of men, that they took down those that were condemned and crucified, and buried them before the going down of the sun."

          1.8.2.1.1.5. Pro:
          The burial could have been a "decoy burial" (link.springer.com [757]).

            1.8.2.1.1.5.1. Pro:
            A decoy burial would explain why the linens were left behind (link.springer.com [758]).

              1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1. Pro:
              The graveclothes were left behind in the tomb.

                1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.1. Con:
                The gospels cannot be relied upon to confirm this fact, since the empty graveclothes, like the empty tomb itself, point to a miraculous event, and one the gospel authors have a reason to emphasize.

                1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2. Con:
                Surely Jesus didn't rise naked from the tomb. Thus, if he rose from the dead, he would have kept his graveclothes in order to leave the tomb in a dignified way.

                  1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1. Con:
                  Given the miraculous nature of the resurrection story, he could have easily given himself fresh clothes.

                    1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
                    At no point in any if the gospels does Jesus create matter from nothing. Thus there is no reason to think he did or could have done so here.

                      1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
                      This is an argument from silence. There is no reason why this can't happen to be the only miracle that involves Jesus creating matter from nothing.

                        1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                        Other miracles Jesus did are unique.

                          1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                          Turning water into wine [759] (John 2:1-12 [760]) is the only miracle that involved transforming one substance into another.

                          1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
                          Calming the storm [761] (Matthew 8:23-27 [762], Mark 4:35-41 [763]) is the only miracle Jesus did that involves controlling the weather.

                      1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.2. Con:
                      Jesus actually did create matter from nothing elsewhere in the gospels.

                        1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.2.1. Pro:
                        Jesus changed water into wine (John 2:1-12 [764]), which requires generating carbon atoms (water has no carbon but wine does).

                          1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
                          The relevant atoms and molecules can be produced from combining the subatomic particles present in water.

                        1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.2.2. Pro:
                        Jesus multiplied food (Matthew 14:13-21 [765]), which is similar to creating matter from nothing.

                          1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.2.2.1. Con:
                          Multiplying or expanding something is essentially different than creating it from thin air.

                        1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.2.3. Pro:
                        According to John 1:3 [766] Jesus created everything that exists.

                          1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.2.1.1.2.3.1. Con:
                          This happens prior to the terrestrial life of Jesus as recorded in the Gospels.

                1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.3. Con:
                The claim that the graveclothes were left behind may not have actually happened and only been a dramatic embellishment added later.

                1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.4. Pro:
                Luke 24:12 [767] says the graveclothes were left behind: "... Bending over, he saw the strips of linen lying by themselves, and he went away, wondering to himself what had happened."

                  1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.4.1. Con:
                  The passage in Luke may be "an interpolation from John" (richardcarrier.info [768]). If this is true, it shouldn't count as independent evidence of the linens being left behind.

                1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.5. Pro:
                John 20:6-8 [769] says the graveclothes were left behind: "...He saw the strips of linen lying there, as well as the cloth that had been wrapped around Jesus’ head. The cloth was still lying in its place, separate from the linen..."

        1.8.2.1.2. Pro:
        Absent the empty tomb story, the empty tomb could only be inferred from the appearances to the disciples. This left many questions unanswered and was not very satisfying.

          1.8.2.1.2.1. Con:
          This was probably not the intent of the empty tomb story because the current account leaves many questions unanswered.

            1.8.2.1.2.1.1. Pro:
            The story doesn't give the exact time Jesus was resurrected.

              1.8.2.1.2.1.1.1. Con:
              As the women came "early in the morning on the third day", Jesus presumably resurrected shortly before then, or sometime during the preceding night.

            1.8.2.1.2.1.2. Pro:
            The story doesn't describe how Jesus got new clothes after removing the graveclothes.

              1.8.2.1.2.1.2.1. Con:
              The story doesn't describe Jesus' clothes (or lack thereof) at all. He may have been naked or miracled himself some clothes.

            1.8.2.1.2.1.3. Con:
            Every account will leave some questions unanswered. The accounts in the gospels answer some pertinent questions, albeit not every question.

          1.8.2.1.2.2. Pro:
          Such questions as "what happened to his body? How did they know it was on the third day? What about the tomb? Who saw Jesus first? How did they know it was the same Jesus and not a phantom, copy, or impostor?" would have gone unanswered, but these are answered by the empty tomb story, even if others are raised or left unanswered.

        1.8.2.1.3. Pro:
        As many early Christians were women, the empty tomb story offers an opportunity for women to be an important part of Jesus' story, from which women are otherwise all but absent.

          1.8.2.1.3.1. Con:
          Women could have been falsely added to an existing story instead.

            1.8.2.1.3.1.1. Con:
            If the empty tomb story is fabricated, they were: they were added to the post mortem appearance stories (from which they are otherwise absent).

          1.8.2.1.3.2. Con:
          Many women encounter Jesus throughout the gospels.

            1.8.2.1.3.2.1. Con:
            They don't play any important part in the story: all their roles are very minor ones.

            1.8.2.1.3.2.2. Pro:
            In Luke 8:1-3 [770] several women are explicitly named as accompanying Jesus in his ministry.

          1.8.2.1.3.3. Con:
          Mary plays a prominent role in the nativity stories.

            1.8.2.1.3.3.1. Con:
            Mary's role is primarily as God's incubator.

              1.8.2.1.3.3.1.1. Con:
              While mostly obscured from the details, Mary also served to parent Jesus for 30 years, which even for good kids becomes a parent's primary role after birth.

                1.8.2.1.3.3.1.1.1. Con:
                This is only implicit in the NT: Mary's parenting is all but absent from the text of the NT. The empty tomb story gives women agency and importance explicitly within the text of the NT.

              1.8.2.1.3.3.1.2. Pro:
              Mary's role is only as mother and parent. But women are more than only mothers and parents: they can exercise autonomy and agency that is not merely related to their reproductive and child-rearing functions. The empty tomb story allows women to have a role that goes beyond their biological or maternal functions and gives them independent agency.

              1.8.2.1.3.3.1.3. Con:
              Mary is the Immaculately Conceived, Perpetually Virgin, Bodily Assumed, Comediatrix and Coredemptrix who is the Queen of Heaven in the New Testament.

                1.8.2.1.3.3.1.3.1. Con:
                All or most of this is not derived from what is written in the NT, but from external sources or traditions.

            1.8.2.1.3.3.2. Con:
            The empty tomb story allows for ordinary women (ones not miraculously and virginally impregnated by God or immaculately conceived) to have a role in the story. As most women are such ordinary women, rather than specially divinely chosen women, the story allows them also to have a role.

          1.8.2.1.3.4. Con:
          This doesn't explain why the empty tomb story was elaborated to include prominent narratives involving male disciples too.

            1.8.2.1.3.4.1. Con:
            This allows the male disciples to be initially skeptical, making their later belief more meaningful.

          1.8.2.1.3.5. Pro:
          This gives an explanation why there would be motivation to fabricate the empty tomb story, or have it arise as a rumor, and why people would be inclined to believe it once they heard it.

            1.8.2.1.3.5.1. Pro:
            If one is biased to think a story is true (for example, if it's satisfying or makes one feel good, is flattering, confirms what one already believes or wants to believe) one won't expend much or any effort to verify it, but will be happy to give it the benefit of the doubt.

              1.8.2.1.3.5.1.1. Pro:
              This same phenomenon can be seen in modern fake news, widely circulated but erroneous articles, racist prejudices, urban legends, the Mandela effect, and common misconceptions.

                1.8.2.1.3.5.1.1.1. Pro:
                Popular belief is sometimes wrong.

                  1.8.2.1.3.5.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                  Arguing that something is true because it is popularly believed is a fallacy called the ad populum fallacy.

        1.8.2.1.4. Pro:
        There is no evidence for the empty tomb stories outside the gospels.

          1.8.2.1.4.1. Pro:
          Paul's epistles, which were written decades before the gospels, contain no traces of an empty tomb story, suggesting the stories were later inventions.

            1.8.2.1.4.1.1. Con:
            1 Corinthians 15:3-4 [771] implies the most important part of the empty tomb story for establishing the historicity of the Resurrection: the idea that Jesus's body was walking around instead of in his grave.

              1.8.2.1.4.1.1.1. Con:
              This doesn't include anyone finding the tomb empty (the empty tomb story), but can be inferred only from the post mortem appearances.

          1.8.2.1.4.2. Con:
          -> See 1.2.7.13.

        1.8.2.1.5. Con:
        All four gospels (Matthew 27:58-60 [772], Mark 15:46 [773], Luke 23:53 [774], John 19:41-42 [775]) state that he was buried in a tomb.

          1.8.2.1.5.1. Con:
          The early development of Christianity demanded the early adoption of the tomb narrative in order to supply much-needed evidence for the bodily resurrection of Jesus. As the tomb narrative was thus a desideratum among the early Christians, the gospels cannot be taken as reliable, unbiased testimonies to the claim of the tomb and honorable burial.

          1.8.2.1.5.2. Con:
          The gospels are not independent: scholars generally agree that Matthew and Luke used Mark as a source. Thus, there are at most 2 independent sources given.

            1.8.2.1.5.2.1. Pro:
            John was the last gospel written and almost surely takes the outline of the story from those that were circulating in the first century, influenced by the other, earlier gospels.

          1.8.2.1.5.3. Con:
          None of Paul's epistles mention an empty tomb. As many of the epistles are significantly earlier (30-40 years or so) than the gospels, this strongly suggests the empty tomb story was developed after Paul.

            1.8.2.1.5.3.1. Pro:
            Paul's epistles do not mention the empty tomb.

              1.8.2.1.5.3.1.1. Con:
              Paul (1 Corinthians 15:14) And if Christ has not been raised, then our preaching is in vain and your faith is in vain.

                1.8.2.1.5.3.1.1.1. Con:
                Being raised doesn't imply a tomb: he could be raised from a common grave.

              1.8.2.1.5.3.1.2. Pro:
              Paul only mentions that Jesus rose from the dead. However, this doesn't itself entail that Jesus was buried in a marked tomb, with an intact corpse, or that his tomb was later found empty.

                1.8.2.1.5.3.1.2.1. Pro:
                For instance, his rising could be merely inferred from the fact that some of his followers had experiences of what they understood to be the risen Jesus.

              1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3. Con:
              Paul's statement in 1 Corinthians 15:3 [776] implies an empty tomb: "... that Christ died for our sins according to the Scriptures, that he was buried, that he was raised on the third day according to the Scriptures ..."

                1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.1. Con:
                The empty tomb is a plot element of every one of the Gospels. Namely, the empty tomb is the claim that a few (three, perhaps) days after Jesus was crucified, someone went to his tomb and found the entrance open and the body missing. This is never mentioned in Paul's epistles.

                  1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.1.1. Pro:
                  From Paul's letters, we may only infer an empty tomb, but nowhere is that explicitly stated. Each of the gospels states it explicitly.

                  1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.1.2. Con:
                  Paul didn't explicitly mention the empty tomb because unlike the gospels, the purpose of his writing wasn't primarily to provide a detailed narrative of Jesus's life.

                1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.2. Pro:
                Similar passages [777] involving the "died ... buried ... raised ... appeared" formula imply an empty tomb.

                  1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.2.1. Con:
                  -> See 1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.1.1.

                  1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.2.2. Con:
                  It could be that [778] "Mark is adopting Paul's language, and thus Mark may have added any new ideology to the simpler core belief of Paul. This therefore tells us nothing about what Paul believed."

                    1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.2.2.1. Con:
                    "In response, however, this renders it particularly implausible that Mark did not narrate the appearance to Peter, the 12, et al., which were essential parts of the very short creed Mark is allegedly elaborating on." (beliefmap.org [779]).

                1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.3. Pro:
                If Paul did not mean that there was an empty tomb, he would have said "appeared" rather than "raised." (beliefmap.org [780])

                  1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.3.1. Con:
                  -> See 1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.1.1.

                1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4. Con:
                1 Corinthians 15:3-11 may be a "post-Pauline interpolation [781]."

                  1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.1. Pro:
                  The burden of proof is on those who wish to show there were no interpolations in this passage. (infidels.org [782])

                    1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.1.1. Con:
                    The burden of proof is actually on those who argue that a specific excerpt from the text (1 Corinthians 15:3-11) is an interpolation, as opposed to 12-19, 20-28, or 29-34.

                  1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.2. Pro:
                  If Paul wrote in 1 Corinthians 15:3 [783] "For I delivered to you as of first importance what I also received ..." (which is "technical language for the handing on of rabbinical tradition [784]"), this would contradict his statement in Galatians 1:11-12 [785] that "...I did not receive it from any man, nor was I taught it..." Thus, Paul did not author 1 Corinthians 15:3.

                    1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.2.1. Con:
                    The point of Galatians 1:11-12 [786] is that "the gospel that was preached by me is not man's gospel." Paul's point is that his teachings ultimately originate with God, not man.

                  1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.3. Con:
                  It would have been very difficult for someone to interpolate text into 1 Corinthians 15 after Paul because of the huge number of copies that would have to be modified.

                    1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.3.1. Con:
                    It could have been interpolated relatively early, prior to the mass copying and dissemination.

                    1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.3.2. Pro:
                    There are tens of thousands [787] of New Testament manuscripts in various languages available.

                  1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.4. Con:
                  There is no manuscript evidence whatsoever [788] to support this claim.

                    1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.4.1. Con:
                    The manuscripts lacking the interpolation may have been (purposefully) destroyed or lost.

                  1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.5. Pro:
                  The appearance to the 500 in 1 Corinthians 15:6 [789] would probably have appeared in the gospels if it was genuine, but it did not. Thus, 1 Corinthians 15:6 is probably a later interpolation [790].

                    1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.5.1. Con:
                    Jesus's appearance on the mountaintop in Matthew 28:16-20 [791] might be the same as the appearance to the 500 from 1 Corinthians 15 [792].

              1.8.2.1.5.3.1.4. Con:
              Paul believed in the physical resurrection of Jesus, which would entail an empty tomb.

                1.8.2.1.5.3.1.4.1. Con:
                -> See 1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.1.

                1.8.2.1.5.3.1.4.2. Con:
                If Jesus had been cremated or buried in a common grave, he could have risen from the dead, yet there would not be an empty tomb. Thus, physical resurrection doesn't itself imply an empty tomb.

                1.8.2.1.5.3.1.4.3. Pro:
                Paul believed that the resurrection of Jesus and believers was/would be physical rather than spiritual.

        1.8.2.1.6. Con:
        The Jewish leaders would not have allowed a crucifixion victim like Jesus to go unburied, and if this is the case Pilate would probably not have gone against the wishes of the Jewish leaders in this regard. Thus, Jesus was buried.

          1.8.2.1.6.1. Con:
          Pontius Pilate is multiply attested as a harsh and ruthless figure, not at all known for his favor to Jewish sensitivities [793]. Thus, a special act of leniency would be out of character and highly unlikely.

          1.8.2.1.6.2. Pro:
          The Jewish leaders would not have allowed a crucifixion victim like Jesus to go unburied.

            1.8.2.1.6.2.1. Con:
            The Jewish leaders themselves condemned Jesus and were not in any position to demand exceptions from standard Roman crucifixion practices, which did not involve a burial.

              1.8.2.1.6.2.1.1. Pro:
              -> See 1.8.2.1.1.4.

            1.8.2.1.6.2.2. Pro:
            The Torah mandates that crucifixion victims be buried [794] (Deuteronomy 21:22-23 [795]).

            1.8.2.1.6.2.3. Pro:
            Ezekiel 39:14-16 [796] tells Jews that to "cleanse the land" it is important for dead bodies to be buried.

            1.8.2.1.6.2.4. Pro:
            Tobit 1:16-18 [797] shows how Jews at the time considered burying abandoned dead bodies to be a highly charitable act [798].

            1.8.2.1.6.2.5. Pro:
            John 19:31 [799] reports, "... Because the Jewish leaders did not want the bodies left on the crosses during the Sabbath ..."

            1.8.2.1.6.2.6. Pro:
            A quote from Josephus implies that Jewish leaders buried crucified criminals.

              1.8.2.1.6.2.6.1. Pro:
              -> See 1.8.2.1.1.4.4.2.1.

          1.8.2.1.6.3. Pro:
          Pilate would not have gone against the wishes of the Jewish leaders regarding the burial of Jesus.

            1.8.2.1.6.3.1. Pro:
            In Matthew 27:11-26 [800] the Jewish leaders persuade Pilate to crucify Jesus, even though Pilate initially wanted to crucify Barabbas instead.

        1.8.2.1.7. Pro:
        The gospel accounts of the empty tomb story are highly inconsistent and show clear signs of motivated modification to match with the intended theology and christology.

          1.8.2.1.7.1. Con:
          Since theological and Christological aspects of Christianity were not actually "formed" until the Nicaean councils [801], if their motivation was to match theology and Christology, those uneducated fisherman failed miserably.

            1.8.2.1.7.1.1. Con:
            Theology and christology are integral aspects of Christianity which can be found in nearly every book of the New Testament, (though not always in agreement with one another). These ideas go back at least as far as St Paul [802](30s-60s CE), who was a well-educated and literate former- pharisee, not a fisherman, and even into pre-Christian second-temple Jusaism.

          1.8.2.1.7.2. Pro:
          For example, Mark's gospel has a theology and Christology closer to St Paul's and so has the corpse disappear in the manner of an apotheosis like Romulus [803] or Augustus Caesar. [804] In contrast, Luke has a much more corporeal resurrection in line with his less cosmic proto-orthodox Christology.

          1.8.2.1.7.3. Con:
          The empty tomb is consistent in all 4 gospels. The crucifixion is also consistent. Many of the teachings are uniform as well. This would have been expected if those consistent things really happened.

          1.8.2.1.7.4. Pro:
          In Mark 16:5, a seated young man greets the visitors (likely the same young man mentioned in Mark 14:51). In Matthew 28:2-5 an angel descends from heaven. In Luke 24:4 two standing men greet the women. In John 20:12, two seated angels appear only after the tomb has first been examined and found deserted. Thus, the story is not consistent across the gospels.

            1.8.2.1.7.4.1. Con:
            Just like with eye witness testimony, we compare accounts, and where they conflict, we are then forced into positions about impossible/possible events. Since its possible for people to be various forms of sitting or standing over the course of a conversation, this point is hardly an inconsistency.

            1.8.2.1.7.4.2. Con:
            Just like with eye witness testimony, we compare accounts, and where they conflict, we are then forced into positions about impossible/possible events. The bible is purposely ambiguous about the angel/man distinction [805]. Therefore that one call the beings an angel and another calls the beings men, is not an inconsistency that would devalue the story

            1.8.2.1.7.4.3. Con:
            Just like with eye witness testimony, we compare accounts, and where they conflict, we are then forced into positions about impossible/possible events. Being that some accounts detail one or 2 beings is a triviality if we consider that beings could be inside, or outside the tomb, coming down, or already down. It would be reasonable in finite space-time that the number of beings would be dependent on the location of the viewer and the time of the viewing.

        1.8.2.1.8. Con:
        Jesus received an honorable burial, which means that he was placed in a tomb.

          1.8.2.1.8.1. Pro:
          Jesus received an honorable burial.

            1.8.2.1.8.1.1. Con:
            The only evidence that we have of this is late, (20-40 years) and at least second hand accounts. Not early- eyewitness testimony

            1.8.2.1.8.1.2. Pro:
            1 Corinthians 15:3-5 [806] is a very old tradition teaching Jesus's burial.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.2.1. Con:
              Burial- yes, but it says nothing about where Jesus was buried. The word used it consistent with a tomb, or an unmarked grave. Its ambiguous

                1.8.2.1.8.1.2.1.1. Pro:
                The passage doesn't include any account of the tomb being found empty: it only says Jesus was buried and then rose and appeared to his followers.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.2.2. Con:
              -> See 1.8.2.1.5.3.1.3.4.

            1.8.2.1.8.1.3. Pro:
            Mark 15:45-47 [807] is a very old (50-70 CE) tradition supporting Jesus's honorable burial.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.3.1. Con:
              This is a 20 to 40 years after the event reportedly took place.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.3.2. Con:
              Paul's epistles are earlier and don't mention an honorable burial.

            1.8.2.1.8.1.4. Pro:
            William Lane Craig [808] writes, "As a member of the Sanhedrin, which condemned Jesus, Joseph of Arimathea is unlikely to be a Christian invention." This is because he is depicted of caring for Jesus's body well despite being a member of a group opposed to believers.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.4.1. Con:
              He could have been invented precisely to support the claim that Jesus was buried in a tomb, rather than left on the cross to rot and for scavenging animals to eat, as was the standard Roman practice.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.4.2. Con:
              The New Testament routinely includes and elevates unlikely and reviled persons, such as prostitutes, tax collector's, centurions, adulterers, lepers, and even Pilate himself.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.4.3. Con:
              Early Christianity was merely a Jewish sect. All of the earliest Christians (Jesus included) were Jews.

            1.8.2.1.8.1.5. Pro:
            According to William Lane Craig [809], "No other competing burial story exists."

              1.8.2.1.8.1.5.1. Pro:
              Legendary burial stories like those of "such pagan figures as Osiris and Empedocles [810]" tend to have a variety of competing versions, because there is no underlying historical fact.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.5.2. Con:
              -> See 1.8.2.1.1.2.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.5.3. Con:
              -> See 1.8.2.1.1.3.

            1.8.2.1.8.1.6. Pro:
            The burial narratives in Acts, 1 Corinthians, and Mark have the same overall structure [811], enhancing their credibility.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.6.1. Con:
              They must have some similar structure in order to achieve the same narrative goal, namely, to establish that Jesus was placed securely in a tomb from which he could later be found to be absent, thereby establishing the resurrection.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.6.2. Con:
              The author of Mark had likely heard of the narrative of 1 Corinthians, making the similarities non-corroborative. The author of Acts was the same as that of Luke, which likely used Mark as source material. Thus, the three sources are not at all independent and their similarity doesn't enhance their credibility.

            1.8.2.1.8.1.7. Con:
            As Jesus was a Galilean peasant executed as a criminal, it is unlikely that he would have received an honorable burial.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.7.1. Con:
              Jesus likely would have received lackluster treatment except a benefactor stepped in [812] and gave him an honorable burial.

              1.8.2.1.8.1.7.2. Pro:
              In general, an executed criminal would have been unceremoniously placed in a dirt grave [813].

      1.8.2.2. Con:
      If the intent of the empty tomb stories was to provide drama to the account, we would expect to see a detailed narrative of the resurrection itself (Jesus leaving the tomb accompanied by all sorts of miracles).

        1.8.2.2.1. Con:
        Adding some drama doesn't require adding arbitrarily large amounts of drama.

        1.8.2.2.2. Con:
        Such a story would gain drama at the expense of credibility.

    1.8.3. Con:
    The only account of the empty tomb is in The Bible, which is an unreliable text.

      1.8.3.1. Pro:
      -> See 1.8.1.1.6.1.

      1.8.3.2. Con:
      -> See 1.2.7.13.

    1.8.4. Pro:
    If his tomb was not empty, critics of Christianity would have refuted Christianity by producing the body. However, this did not happen, so the tomb must have been empty.

      1.8.4.1. Con:
      By "Pentecost, when the disciples made the first public statement about the resurrection [814]", the body was decomposed enough that even if critics of Christianity did produce the body, they could not easily prove that it was really the body of Jesus.

        1.8.4.1.1. Con:
        They would have at least been able to prove that the tomb was still closed up (tektonics.org [815]).

        1.8.4.1.2. Con:
        The authorities could have publicly opened the tomb with witnesses around and found a body there. Since the disciples knew where the tomb was the presence of the body in the tomb could have disproven Christianity, even if the body itself was difficult to identify. (tektonics.org [816])

          1.8.4.1.2.1. Con:
          Since the resurrection was not publicly proclaimed until pentecost, there would have been ample time for the body to have been moved.

            1.8.4.1.2.1.1. Con:
            Having the opportunity, (7 weeks,) isn't sufficient to imbue guilt as there were also 7 weeks of time to ensure that nefarious activities were investigated and punished.

          1.8.4.1.2.2. Con:
          Jesus may have been placed in a common dirt grave and never placed in a tomb. So examining the tomb would have been pointless.

            1.8.4.1.2.2.1. Con:
            This is pure speculation, you might as well have offered that Jesus was beamed up to the mothership. As such this claim offers no evidence, insight or value to the discussion at hand.

      1.8.4.2. Con:
      Critics of Christianity were not motivated enough to try to produce the body.

        1.8.4.2.1. Pro:
        It could be that, "with the leader of the sect dead [817]" non-Christians did not see the early Christian movement as a threat and thus would not have expended any effort to try and refute it.

        1.8.4.2.2. Con:
        -> See 1.3.8.1.1.5.4.1.

      1.8.4.3. Pro:
      Matthew 28:13 [818] records the chief priests trying to argue that the body was stolen rather than simply producing the body, which would have been unlikely if the body was still there.

      1.8.4.4. Pro:
      Jesus received an honorable burial, which means that his burial location was known and claims of an empty tomb could have been easily verified.

        1.8.4.4.1. Con:
        The fact that his burial place was known also would allow a definite location from which to take it.

        1.8.4.4.2. Con:
        The fact that his burial place was known also would allow an effective moving of the body since no one would look for it elsewhere.

        1.8.4.4.3. Pro:
        -> See 1.8.2.1.8.1.

      1.8.4.5. Con:
      Sometimes religious movements persevere even though they have been proven wrong. Therefore, we cannot argue that Christianity has not been falsified in this fashion simply because it exists today.

        1.8.4.5.1. Pro:
        The Millerites [819] predicted that the world would end by 1844. However, offshoots of this group such as the seventh day adventists [820] still exist today.

          1.8.4.5.1.1. Con:
          This group still gave up the core belief that had been falsified (the idea that the world would end by 1844). Therefore, this is not an example of a religious group continuing to hold a clearly falsified belief.

    1.8.5. Con:
    If the resurrection was spiritual, the tomb would not need to be empty. Thus, resurrection doesn't necessarily imply an empty tomb.

      1.8.5.1. Con:
      Except that is what Mary and Martha found when they entered the tomb. That the body of Jesus was not there. [821]

    1.8.6. Pro:
    -> See 1.8.1.1.5.3.

    1.8.7. Con:
    The empty tomb story is probably a later invention because it was missing from "early Christian writers like Paul [822]."

      1.8.7.1. Con:
      Even if Paul did not mention the empty tomb story in detail, this may be because he simply chose not to emphasize that fact in his teaching, not because he was unaware of it.

      1.8.7.2. Con:
      Early Christian creedal statements show that just because an author doesn't explicitly mention the empty tomb, it doesn't follow that they were unaware of it or denied it.

        1.8.7.2.1. Pro:
        Other Christian creedal statements do not explicitly mention the empty tomb [823] even though the communities saying these creeds were well aware of the Gospel accounts. The empty tomb isn't explicitly mentioned not because the creed authors were unaware of it, but because it was implied.

          1.8.7.2.1.1. Pro:
          The Apostles' Creed [824] does not explicitly mention people visiting the tomb and finding it empty, although that was clearly believed by that time.

          1.8.7.2.1.2. Pro:
          The Nicene Creed [825] does not explicitly mention people visiting the empty tomb of Jesus, although that was clearly believed by that time.

          1.8.7.2.1.3. Pro:
          The Athanasian Creed [826] does not explicitly describe people visiting the empty tomb of Jesus, but that was clearly believed by this time.

          1.8.7.2.1.4. Con:
          We have other reasons for believing that the authors of these creedal statements believed the empty tomb story, and this overrides the fact that they didn't mention it. The same is not true of Paul.

            1.8.7.2.1.4.1. Pro:
            All the creeds were written hundreds of years after the gospels were written and disseminated. Thus, creed authors would have been well aware of the empty tomb stories in the gospels, but Paul would not have known them.

              1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1. Con:
              Paul was aware of the gospel of Luke, and by extension the empty tomb story.

                1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
                1 Timothy 5:18 [827] quotes Luke 10:7 [828].

                  1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.1. Con:
                  This is ambiguous. Paul could have been quoting Leviticus 19:13 [829] or Deuteronomy 24:14 [830] instead.

                    1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                    The Greek phrases in 1 Timothy 5:18 [831] and Luke 10:7 [832] are extraordinarily similar, with the former saying "axios ho ergates tou misthou autou" and the latter saying "axios gar ho ergates tou misthou autou." The only difference is a single instance of "gar," meaning "for." Neither Leviticus 19:13 [833] nor Deuteronomy 24:14 [834] come close to this level of similarity.

                  1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2. Con:
                  1 Timothy may not have been written by Paul. [835]

                    1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
                    Quotes from 1 Timothy appear in very early writings, suggesting it was very early.

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
                      The Didache, written around (50-120 AD [836]), quotes 1 Timothy 3:4 (Chapter 15 [837]).

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.1.2. Con:
                      The text may be both early and non-Pauline.

                    1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.2. Con:
                    Early church fathers attribute 1 Timothy to Paul.

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.2.1. Pro:
                      Polycarp (69-156 [838]) includes 1 Timothy 6:10 and 1 Timothy 6:7 in a list of Pauline quotes (Epistle to the Philippians 3-4 [839]).

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.2.2. Pro:
                      Irenaeus attributes the 1 and 2 Timothy to Paul (Against Heresies 3:3:3 [840]).

                    1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.3. Pro:
                    Paul's other letters do not describe the churches as having pastors and overseers like 1 Timothy does (e.g. 1 Timothy 3:1 [841]).

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.3.1. Con:
                      Philippians 1:1 [842] mentions "overseers and deacons."

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.3.2. Con:
                      1 Thessalonians 5:12 [843] refers to those "over you in the Lord," which probably refers to church leaders.

                    1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.4. Pro:
                    The literary style and vocabulary [844] of 1 Timothy does not match Paul's other letters.

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.4.1. Con:
                      Paul is known to have dictated his letters to a scribe (Romans 16:22 [845]). If this was the case for 1 Timothy then the scribe's literary style could have been incorporated into the text.

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.4.2. Con:
                      This could simply be because Paul is discussing a variety of topics, with different topics requiring different vocabulary.

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.4.3. Con:
                      Unlike most of his other letters, in 1 Timothy Paul is writing to a single individual rather than a congregation [846], which could encourage the use of a different vocabulary or style.

                        1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.4.3.1. Con:
                        This difference also might simply identify it as non-Pauline.

                          1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.4.3.1.1. Con:
                          -> See 1.5.3.3.6.6.1.1.

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.4.4. Con:
                      -> See 1.5.3.3.6.6.1.1.

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.1.2.4.5. Pro:
                      Critics argue [847] that "1 and 2 Timothy have grammatical forms, such as how definite articles, prepositions, and conjunctions are used, that are unique to the Pastoral Epistles and lack grammatical forms typical of Paul's other ten epistles."

                1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2. Con:
                Most scholars believe the book of Luke-Acts was written after the death of Paul.

                  1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.1. Pro:
                  Paul died [848] around 60-65 AD.

                  1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.2. Pro:
                  Luke was written after 70 AD.

                    1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.2.1. Pro:
                    Luke appears [849] to "address concerns raised by the destruction of the Temple in 70 CE."

                    1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.2.2. Con:
                    Luke records [850] Jesus's prediction of the destruction of the Temple (Luke 21:5-6 [851]), but not its fulfilment in 70 AD. If Luke wrote his gospel after the destruction of the temple, he would have cited its destruction as a fulfillment of Jesus's prophecy.

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.2.2.1. Con:
                      Citing the fulfillment of a prophecy within the text itself would have made the prophecy itself seem inauthentic and post-hoc. The point is to make the text seem to be written before the event prophesied took place, so as to seem prophetic.

                        1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.2.2.1.1. Con:
                        If this is the case, then it is unclear why the fulfillments of some of Jesus's other prophecies are explicitly stated in the gospels.

                          1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.2.2.1.1.1. Con:
                          The alleged fulfillment of those prophecies happened during Jesus' lifetime.

                          1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.2.2.1.1.2. Pro:
                          Jesus predicts Peter's threefold denial in Matthew 26:34 [852], and this is explicitly fulfilled by Matthew 26:75 [853].

                        1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.2.2.1.2. Pro:
                        The book of Daniel (well known to Jesus and his followers) frames itself as written in the 6th century BCE, making prophecies about events that would not happen for hundreds of years. However, modern scholarship places it in the 2nd century BCE, with many of the prophecies having already occurred. Thus, the prophecies that had already occurred are framed as being in the author's future, and this lends credibility to other prophecies further in the future.

                          1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.2.2.1.2.1. Pro:
                          Daniel was written in the 2nd century BCE.

                            1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.2.2.2.1.2.1.1. Pro:
                            -> See 1.8.1.2.1.7.1.4.5.1.

                1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.3. Pro:
                Paul's reference to "the brother who is praised by all the churches for his service to the gospel" (2 Corinthians 8:18 [854]) is understood as Luke and the gospel he wrote by ancient sources [855]. If this is true, Paul must be aware of Luke's gospel.

                  1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.3.1. Pro:
                  Origen writes "And the third by Luke, the Gospel [856] commended by Paul [857], and composed for Gentile converts" (Church History 6:25:6 [858]).

                  1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.3.2. Con:
                  Many scholars do not believe [859] that Luke is the real author of the gospel commonly attributed to him. If that is true, then 2 Corinthians doesn't refer to what is known as the Gospel of Luke.

                    1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.3.2.1. Con:
                    Luke wrote Luke.

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.3.2.1.1. Pro:
                      The usage of first person "we" in the book of Acts (e.g. Acts 16:11-15 [860]) suggests that Acts was written by one of Paul's companions, one of whom was Luke (Colossians 4:14 [861]). Since Luke and Acts were probably written by the same person, this supports the idea that Luke wrote Luke.

                        1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.3.2.1.1.1. Con:
                        The book of Colossians was probably a forgery and should not be relied on.

                          1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.3.2.1.1.1.1. Con:
                          -> See 1.7.4.6.7.1.1.

                        1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.3.2.1.1.2. Con:
                        This could merely be a literary technique to lend immediate credibility to the narrative.

                      1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.3.2.1.2. Pro:
                      The Muratorian Canon [862] states, "The third book of the Gospel [is that] according to Luke. Luke, "the" physician, after the ascension of Christ, when Paul had taken him with him as a companion of his traveling, [and after he had made] an investigation, wrote in his own name..." (3-6).

                  1.8.7.2.1.4.1.1.3.3. Pro:
                  Jerome writes, "An adherent of the apostle [863] Paul [864], and companion of all his journeying, [Luke] wrote a Gospel [865], concerning which the same Paul [866] says, 'We send with him a brother whose praise in the gospel is among all the churches'" (Lives of Illustrious Men 7 [867]).

      1.8.7.3. Pro:
      We reject the historicity of other events because a person or persons we expect to have mentioned it did not, in fact, mention it. Thus, it is reasonable to reject the historicity of the empty tomb because Paul didn't mention it.

        1.8.7.3.1. Pro:
        Non-Muslims reject the historicity of the splitting of the moon on the basis that if it happened, there would be way more written documentation of the event than there is.

          1.8.7.3.1.1. Con:
          Here, the historicity of the splitting of the moon is rejected because many thousands of people who probably would have mentioned it didn't, not because a single individual failed to mention it. Thus, it is not analogous to rejecting the historicity of the empty tomb because Paul didn't mention it.

            1.8.7.3.1.1.1. Con:
            The analogy is the same: persons we expect to mention it don't mention it. There is no evidence that anyone prior to Mark mentions it.

              1.8.7.3.1.1.1.1. Con:
              Rejecting an event because "people" (plural) failed to mention it is not analogous to rejecting an event because a single person failed to mention it. A single person failing to mention an event provides far less evidence against it than multiple people failing to mention it.

                1.8.7.3.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                The difference here is one only of degree, not kind. One person may be less evidence than 2 or 5 or 20, but it is more evidence than 0.

                  1.8.7.3.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                  One person not mentioning an event is a negligible amount of evidence against its existence because for nearly any event, one can name a person who didn't mention it.

                    1.8.7.3.1.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                    Paul is not some random person. He is the earliest source on Christianity, the founder of numerous important Churches, and the author of numerous Church teachings.

          1.8.7.3.1.2. Con:
          Non-Muslims may have many other reasons for rejecting the historicity of the splitting of the moon.

            1.8.7.3.1.2.1. Pro:
            Non-Muslims may reject the historicity of the splitting of the moon on the basis that if the moon was split, there would be a huge crack going around the entire moon that would be visible using telescopes.

        1.8.7.3.2. Con:
        -> See 1.8.7.3.1.1.1.1.

        1.8.7.3.3. Con:
        People often describe events without explicitly stating key underlying facts they consider to be self evident. Like describing events at their children's birthday party without formally stating that it is metabolically, neurologically, and culturally possible for human beings to gain pleasure and nutritional benefit from eating cake

      1.8.7.4. Con:
      1 Corinthians 15:3-8 Paul says, "For what I received I passed on to you as of first importance: that Christ died for our sins according to the Scriptures, that he was buried, that he was raised on the third day according to the Scriptures, and that he appeared to Peter, and then to the Twelve. After that, he appeared to more than five hundred of the brothers at the same time, most of whom are still living, though some have fallen asleep. Then he appeared to James, then to all the apostles"

        1.8.7.4.1. Con:
        This passage mentions no tomb, nor an empty tomb.

          1.8.7.4.1.1. Con:
          The "buried" and "raised" part implies that an empty tomb exists, even if it is not explicitly mentioned.

            1.8.7.4.1.1.1. Con:
            As Jesus could have been buried in a common grave, or simply interred in a hole, "buried" does not mean there was an empty tomb in the sense of a cave in which to lay the deceased to decompose in the air. The gospels all allege that Jesus was placed in a stone cave, not interred in a soil grave.

      1.8.7.5. Con:
      Mark clearly mentions the empty tomb.

        1.8.7.5.1. Con:
        Mark was written 20-40 years after Paul's epistles.

        1.8.7.5.2. Pro:
        In Mark 16:6 [868] the man at the tomb says, "...He has risen! He is not here. See the place where they laid him." These statements imply that Jesus is not "in the place where they laid him" i.e. the tomb.

      1.8.7.6. Pro:
      -> See 1.8.2.1.5.3.1.

      1.8.7.7. Pro:
      Peter's sermon in Acts 2 [869] does not mention the empty tomb.

        1.8.7.7.1. Con:
        Acts 2:31 [870] explicitly says, "Seeing what was to come, he spoke of the resurrection of the Messiah, that he was not abandoned to the realm of the dead, nor did his body see decay," implying that Jesus' body is not still decaying in the tomb.

          1.8.7.7.1.1. Con:
          The author of Acts was the same as that of Luke, and very likely had access to the gospel of Mark.

    1.8.8. Pro:
    -> See 1.2.7.13.

    1.8.9. Con:
    An empty tomb is primarily evidence that the body was taken away - this could plausibly have been done by humans.

      1.8.9.1. Pro:
      There are numerous hypothetical scenarios that would explain the empty tomb more plausibly than the resurrection.

        1.8.9.1.1. Pro:
        The guards themselves may have removed the body.

          1.8.9.1.1.1. Con:
          The guards would have had no reason to steal the body.

            1.8.9.1.1.1.1. Con:
            Possible reasons include necromancy, necrophilia, to desecrate the body, or because they (believed they) received an order to do so.

              1.8.9.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
              If the stole the body because they thought they had received an order to do so, and they had learned of their mistake before being caught, they would have returned the body to the tomb to avoid being caught. If they had been caught before learning their mistake, the body would have been found in their possession and recovered.

                1.8.9.1.1.1.1.1.1. Con:
                They may never have learned of the error.

                1.8.9.1.1.1.1.1.2. Con:
                They may have only learned of the error after they had forgotten where they had moved the body to.

                1.8.9.1.1.1.1.1.3. Con:
                They may have learned of the error after the body was already decayed and no longer movable (e.g. 40-80 days later).

        1.8.9.1.2. Con:
        At the time, Jesus was a fairly insignificant person. It is unclear why anyone wanted to steal the body.

          1.8.9.1.2.1. Pro:
          The fact that Jesus was crucified does not make him special: Crucifixion [871] was a very common and frequently used form of capital punishment during that era.

            1.8.9.1.2.1.1. Pro:
            Crucifixion was especially popular in the Roman-occupied Holy Land.

              1.8.9.1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
              The famous Roman general Varus crucified 2,000 Jews [872] in 4 B.C.

              1.8.9.1.2.1.1.2. Pro:
              According to the Roman-Jewish historian Josephus [873], there were mass crucifixions in the first century AD.

          1.8.9.1.2.2. Pro:
          -> See 1.7.4.6.2.1.

          1.8.9.1.2.3. Con:
          There are reasons to move the corpse of even a fairly insignificant person.

            1.8.9.1.2.3.1. Pro:
            Necromancers could have stolen Jesus's body.

              1.8.9.1.2.3.1.1. Pro:
              Necromancers would have sought body parts of people that had died violently.

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.1.1. Pro:
                The Papyri Graecae Magicae [874] say that an ingredient for "some magical inks" is "blood from someone who has died by violence."

              1.8.9.1.2.3.1.2. Con:
              A necromancer would have chosen an easier place to get a corpse for magic, such as a "rural graveyard." (richardcarrier.info [875])

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.2.1. Con:
                A grave would have actually been more difficult to rob than a tomb because of the digging involved. (richardcarrier.info [876])

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.2.2. Con:
                A thief would have found it "easier to remain anonymous and disappear in crowded cosmopolitan streets than in rural areas" (richardcarrier.info [877]).

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.2.3. Con:
                Necromancers would have specifically sought the bodies of convicted criminals, and the best place to find these sorts of bodies would have been in urban areas. (richardcarrier.info [878])

                  1.8.9.1.2.3.1.2.3.1. Pro:
                  Convicted criminals would have been sought by necromancers because they died violently.

                    1.8.9.1.2.3.1.2.3.1.1. Pro:
                    -> See 1.8.9.1.2.3.1.1.

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.2.4. Con:
                Richard Carrier writes, "Tombs would also be more abundant than graves" (richardcarrier.info [879]).

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.2.5. Con:
                The tombs would have been "larger (i.e. containing more bodies behind any given door), near major urban centers," making urban centers an attractive target for necromancers. (richardcarrier.info [880])

              1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3. Con:
              Necromancy would not have been practiced in Judea.

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.1. Con:
                People from Egypt and Libya practiced magic, and there is Biblical evidence of people from Egypt and Libya visiting Judea. (richardcarrier.info [881])

                  1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.1.1. Pro:
                  People from Egypt and Libya practiced magic.

                  1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.1.2. Pro:
                  There is Biblical evidence of people from Egypt and Libya visiting Judea.

                    1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.1.2.1. Pro:
                    Acts 2:10 [882] reports people from Egypt visiting Judea.

                    1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.1.2.2. Pro:
                    Acts 6:9 [883] mentions people from Alexandria, a city in Egypt [884], visiting Judea.

                    1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.1.2.3. Pro:
                    Simon of Cyrene (Cyrene is a city in Libya [885]) came "from a province on the other side of Egypt" (richardcarrier.info [886]).

                      1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.1.2.3.1. Con:
                      The relevant Biblical texts (Mark 15:21 [887], Luke 23:26 [888]) just say that he came "from the country."

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.2. Con:
                The Magi (Matthew 2:1 [889], etc.) were sorcerers.

                  1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.2.1. Pro:
                  The word used for "Magi" in Matthew 2:1 literally means "sorcerer [890]."

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.3. Con:
                The Archaeological Encyclopedia of the Holy Land [891] says that, "sorcerers, magicians and necromancers abounded in Israelite and Judahite society."

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.4. Con:
                Simon Magus practiced sorcery in Samaria (Acts 8:9 [892]).

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.3.5. Con:
                "Sources confirm cases of Jews or foreigners in Palestine being convicted of sorcery" (richardcarrier.info [893]).

              1.8.9.1.2.3.1.4. Con:
              Since sorcery was a capital crime, people would have been unlikely to do it.

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.4.1. Con:
                Historical evidence indicates that many people committed capital crimes in ancient Judea.

                  1.8.9.1.2.3.1.4.1.1. Pro:
                  Jesus himself committed a capital crime, as he was tried and executed.

                  1.8.9.1.2.3.1.4.1.2. Pro:
                  In Mark 15:27 [894] we read that two robbers were crucified next to Jesus.

                  1.8.9.1.2.3.1.4.1.3. Pro:
                  In Mark 15:7 [895] we read that the murderer Barabbas was in custody and could have been sentenced to death.

              1.8.9.1.2.3.1.5. Con:
              Necromancers typically only stole part of the body, not the whole body [896].

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.5.1. Con:
                Richard Carrier writes, "So it would still be safer and more efficient to make off with the whole body, and dissect it at liesure [sic] later, to maximize the booty." (richardcarrier.info [897]).

                  1.8.9.1.2.3.1.5.1.1. Pro:
                  Taking the whole body would have helped with getting the heart and therefore blood of the corpse for magical purposes.

                    1.8.9.1.2.3.1.5.1.1.1. Pro:
                    Necromancers would have wanted to get specifically the heart and/or blood of a corpse like Jesus's.

                      1.8.9.1.2.3.1.5.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                      -> See 1.8.9.1.2.3.1.1.1.

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.5.2. Pro:
                Pliny writes [898], "Apollonius, again, informs us in his writings, that the most effectual remedy for tooth-ache is to scarify the gums with the tooth of a man who has died a violent death."

                1.8.9.1.2.3.1.5.3. Pro:
                J.P. Holding writes [899], "…all the evidence suggests that when necromancers had need of a body for their arts, they did not steal the whole body from the tomb. Instead, they would either conduct their rituals in the tomb itself, leaving the body where it was when they were finished; or, they would slice off whatever they needed mutilating the body and taking the selected parts with them, leaving the rest behind..."

          1.8.9.1.2.4. Con:
          According to the gospels, Jesus had a sign on his cross staying that he was the "king of the Jews". This alone would make Jesus hardly insignificant.

            1.8.9.1.2.4.1. Con:
            The gospels are biased and the authors portray Jesus as they saw him.

          1.8.9.1.2.5. Con:
          Jesus had a considerable following during his life and hence would be quite different than most other people.

            1.8.9.1.2.5.1. Con:
            -> See 1.8.1.1.5.2.1.

            1.8.9.1.2.5.2. Pro:
            If Jesus had not had attracted considerably large crowds, the Romans wouldn't have punished him and regarded him as a political risk.

        1.8.9.1.3. Pro:
        The Jews could have stolen his body.

          1.8.9.1.3.1. Pro:
          The Jews may have stolen the body because they didn't want the site to become "a place of pilgrimage for Jesus' disciples [900]."

            1.8.9.1.3.1.1. Con:
            Arguably, this would have led to "the sort of problem they were trying to avoid - rumors that Jesus had risen. [901]" Surely the Jewish leaders would have been smart enough to anticipate this possibility if they intended to not let the tomb become a pilgrimage site.

              1.8.9.1.3.1.1.1. Con:
              Plans can backfire

                1.8.9.1.3.1.1.1.1. Pro:
                The maccabean conversion of the idumeans led to the rise of Herod the Great, which many Jews regretted happening.

          1.8.9.1.3.2. Con:
          It is very unlikely that someone other than one of Jesus's followers would have stolen the body, because they would have produced the body once it became clear that people were claiming Jesus rose.

            1.8.9.1.3.2.1. Con:
            The disciples only proclaimed Jesus risen 50 days after his burial, by which time the body would have decayed considerably and likely not have been either retrievable or recognizable.

            1.8.9.1.3.2.2. Con:
            It's not clear that even if the decayed remains were produced that anyone would have believed it was the real Jesus.

            1.8.9.1.3.2.3. Con:
            If the body was moved to a mass or unmarked grave, producing the body 50 days later would likely have been very difficult or impossible.

        1.8.9.1.4. Pro:
        The Romans could have stolen the body.

          1.8.9.1.4.1. Con:
          There is no clear reason why they would have done this.

          1.8.9.1.4.2. Con:
          -> See 1.8.9.1.3.2.

        1.8.9.1.5. Pro:
        Joseph of Arimathea could have stolen/taken the body.

          1.8.9.1.5.1. Con:
          -> See 1.8.9.1.3.2.

          1.8.9.1.5.2. Pro:
          He may have always intended the burial to be temporary, just to store it over the the approaching night and Sabbath, then remove it after sundown the following day.
