          1.8.9.1.5.3. Pro:
          He may have independently changed his mind or been prevailed upon to move the body by his family or other members of the council.

          1.8.9.1.5.4. Con:
          If this was the case, Joseph wouldn't have much reason to keep this a secret once rumors got out of a resurrection.

            1.8.9.1.5.4.1. Con:
            He may have tried to spread the word that he moved the body but was simply ignored or disbelieved.

              1.8.9.1.5.4.1.1. Con:
              The non-Christians would have no reason not to believe Joseph.

                1.8.9.1.5.4.1.1.1. Con:
                Non-christians may have believed him, but Christians and those sympathetic to Christianity would likely have just ignored him.

                1.8.9.1.5.4.1.1.2. Pro:
                This idea would have become prevalent in non-Christian literature such as Celsus's works and the Talmud.

                  1.8.9.1.5.4.1.1.2.1. Con:
                  There may not have been any literature about this.

            1.8.9.1.5.4.2. Con:
            The empty tomb story (as distinct from the "rose from the dead" story) may only have gained popularity as much as 40 years after Jesus died. By this time, Joseph of Arimathea may have died or may have forgotten about the incident.

              1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1. Con:
              The "rose from the dead" story logically implies the "empty tomb" story, and people at the time would have known this.

                1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.1. Pro:
                A person's body cannot be two places at once (walking around risen and in a tomb)

                  1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.1.1. Con:
                  Jesus could have gotten a brand new body, leaving behind his former body.

                1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.2. Con:
                The empty tomb story involves numerous elements that are not implied by the "rose from the dead" story.

                  1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.2.1. Pro:
                  That women followers came the morning of the third day is not implied.

                  1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.2.2. Pro:
                  That angel or angels were present is not implied.

                  1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.2.3. Pro:
                  That the stone was moved is not implied.

                  1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.2.4. Con:
                  These elements are all irrelevant to the idea that is most important to the historicity of a Resurrection: that the body was put somewhere but then mysteriously disappeared.

                    1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.2.4.1. Con:
                    What is most important for the resurrection is that Jesus was alive, died, then came back from the dead. That his body moved from where it was initially placed is not itself sufficient to establish that, as it may have moved without returning to life (e.g. someone else may have moved it).

                    1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.2.4.2. Con:
                    What Christians relied on most for establishing the resurrection were the post mortem appearances, not the disappearance of the body, as the body could have disappeared in other ways (e.g. if someone moved it).

                      1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.2.4.2.1. Con:
                      The extent to which Christians relied on the body disappearance story has nothing to do with how early the body disappearance story existed.

                        1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.2.4.2.1.1. Con:
                        It does have to do with it, because the belief originated from somewhere, and if it didn't originate from the empty tomb story, the empty tomb story could have come after what it did originate from.

                  1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.2.5. Pro:
                  The body may never have been laid in any tomb. Other possibilities include being placed in a common dirt grave or left on the cross for scavengers to pick the corpse clean, as was standard Roman practice.

                1.8.9.1.5.4.2.1.3. Pro:
                In discussing how resurrection was understood in early Christianity, Paul describes the body that is "sown" being the same as the body that is "raised" (1 Corinthians 15:42-44 [902]), thus implying that the body of a resurrected person is no longer in the grave.

            1.8.9.1.5.4.3. Con:
            Christianity was a pretty small sect early on. It's possible Joseph of Arimathea simply never heard much about the early Christians and so didn't have any reason to publicize that he moved the body.

              1.8.9.1.5.4.3.1. Con:
              Joseph of Arimathea would probably have known the Christians well as the people constantly contacting him about where Jesus had been buried.

                1.8.9.1.5.4.3.1.1. Con:
                The Christians didn't ask him where the body was moved to: they interpreted the original tomb being empty as indicating that Jesus had risen from the dead.

        1.8.9.1.6. Pro:
        The gardener of the tomb could have removed the body.

          1.8.9.1.6.1. Con:
          -> See 1.8.9.1.3.2.

          1.8.9.1.6.2. Pro:
          The gardener could have removed the body to prevent curious disciples from coming to "trampl[e] over his newly planted lettuce seeds [903]."

            1.8.9.1.6.2.1. Con:
            It is widely speculated that Jesus himself was the gardener [904] due to his mannerisms.

            1.8.9.1.6.2.2. Con:
            This is one account of what happened, making it unreliable.

        1.8.9.1.7. Pro:
        -> See 1.8.9.1.2.3.1.

        1.8.9.1.8. Pro:
        -> See 1.8.1.1.5.2.1.

        1.8.9.1.9. Pro:
        (Non-necromantic) graverobbers could have stolen his body.

          1.8.9.1.9.1. Con:
          If graverobbers did enter Jesus's tomb, they probably would have stolen all the valuables but left the body behind.

        1.8.9.1.10. Pro:
        Jesus's family could have stolen the body.

        1.8.9.1.11. Con:
        The graveclothes were left behind in the tomb, but it is unlikely that a thief would have removed the graveclothes and left them behind. This makes the stolen body hypothesis less credible (beliefmap.org [905]).

          1.8.9.1.11.1. Con:
          If Jesus did indeed rise bodily from the dead, there's no reason why Jesus wouldn't have taken them to clothe himself, at least until he could find some better clothing. Thus the leaving of the graveclothes is an odd feature in either case and can't count only against one position.

          1.8.9.1.11.2. Pro:
          -> See 1.8.2.1.1.5.1.1.

          1.8.9.1.11.3. Pro:
          It is unlikely that a thief would have removed the graveclothes.

            1.8.9.1.11.3.1. Con:
            The thief could have brought other clothes to dress the body of Jesus to cover up the fact that they were carrying a freshly buried body.

              1.8.9.1.11.3.1.1. Con:
              It's more likely they would have just brought a bag to put the body in.

                1.8.9.1.11.3.1.1.1. Pro:
                This would have been a faster way to conceal the body

                1.8.9.1.11.3.1.1.2. Pro:
                They wouldn't have had to touch the gross corpse as directly

                  1.8.9.1.11.3.1.1.2.1. Con:
                  A corpse that is only a day or two old is hardly "gross".

                1.8.9.1.11.3.1.1.3. Con:
                However unlikely it is that they undressed him, it's more likely than that a supernatural resurrection happened.

                1.8.9.1.11.3.1.1.4. Con:
                Carrying a dead body in a bag is suspicious and may put the thieves at risk.

            1.8.9.1.11.3.2. Pro:
            John Chrysostom [906] says, "For if they had been disposed to steal, they would not have stolen the body naked, not because of dishonoring it only, but in order not to delay and lose time in stripping it, ... Especially when it was myrrh, a drug that adheres so to the body, and cleaves to the clothes, whence it was not easy to take the clothes off the body, but they that did this needed much time, so that from this again, the tale of the theft is improbable." (Homily 90, section 2)

            1.8.9.1.11.3.3. Pro:
            "The stench of the putrefying corpse would be needlessly exacerbated." (beliefmap.org [907])

            1.8.9.1.11.3.4. Con:
            A thief might have removed the graveclothes because they only cared about having the corpse, not the linens. (jashow.org [908])

              1.8.9.1.11.3.4.1. Con:
              Even if the thief didn't want to steal the linens, they probably would not have removed them because linens are not very heavy, so removing them wouldn't have made escape easier, and because removing the linens would have cost precious time.

        1.8.9.1.12. Con:
        Thieves would not have attempted a corpse-theft during "the Passover festival, which lasted just a few days, but during which the population of Jerusalem swelled three-fold from all the incoming Jews." (beliefmap.org [909]).

          1.8.9.1.12.1. Con:
          The body may not have been removed by thieves. Roman soldiers, for instance, may have removed it.

          1.8.9.1.12.2. Con:
          The increased population would have increased the number of potential perpetrators and would have diverted scrutiny elsewhere.

        1.8.9.1.13. Con:
        There would have been too little time to successfully steal the body.

          1.8.9.1.13.1. Pro:
          The body was in the tomb for only 40 hours (beliefmap.org [910]).

            1.8.9.1.13.1.1. Con:
            The removal could have been planned and executed within 40 hours.

        1.8.9.1.14. Pro:
        Ancient physicians could have stolen Jesus's body for "anatomical research" (richardcarrier.info [911]).

          1.8.9.1.14.1. Con:
          "Such persons were extraordinarily rare, and generally congregated to areas where there was already a significant medical research community (e.g. Alexandria), and Jerusalem did not qualify" (richardcarrier.info [912]).

        1.8.9.1.15. Con:
        If his body was stolen, it would have required a conspiracy of several motivated individuals, not just a single person. This makes the stolen body hypothesis less likely.

          1.8.9.1.15.1. Con:
          Given the inherent unlikelihood of physical resurrection, positing a somewhat improbable event is not unwarranted.

          1.8.9.1.15.2. Pro:
          -> See 1.4.20.3.

        1.8.9.1.16. Con:
        If the thieves were caught, they would have been executed by law. Since the risks associated with stealing the corpse were so high, it is unlikely anyone would have done it.

          1.8.9.1.16.1. Con:
          Death sentence for a crime is a deterrent, but it does not makes the crime impossible.

          1.8.9.1.16.2. Con:
          -> See 1.8.9.1.2.3.1.4.1.

          1.8.9.1.16.3. Pro:
          -> See 1.8.1.1.5.2.3.

        1.8.9.1.17. Con:
        The tomb was guarded, which means that anyone attempting to steal the body would probably have been caught.

          1.8.9.1.17.1. Con:
          The guards could have been present but asleep.

            1.8.9.1.17.1.1. Con:
            The punishment for Roman guards sleeping on the job is so severe that "if they admitted their sleep they were as good as pronouncing their own death sentences [913]." Thus, it is unlikely that they would have let themselves fall asleep if they were present.

              1.8.9.1.17.1.1.1. Con:
              -> See 1.8.9.1.2.3.1.4.1.

              1.8.9.1.17.1.1.2. Con:
              -> See 1.4.7.1.11.2.1.

            1.8.9.1.17.1.2. Con:
            "Even if the guards had fallen asleep, they would have woken up when the stone was being rolled away." (beliefmap.org [914])

          1.8.9.1.17.2. Con:
          The guards themselves may have moved the body.

          1.8.9.1.17.3. Pro:
          -> See 1.4.7.1.

          1.8.9.1.17.4. Con:
          The guards would have been there to stop Jesus's supporters causing trouble not to stop dead bodies sneaking out of the tomb. They would have been looking the wrong way and looking for the wrong thing. By Saturday night Sunday morning when there was no sign of a crowd of demonstrators the guards had probably stopped paying much attention to anything

          1.8.9.1.17.5. Con:
          The body could have been stolen before the guards arrived.

            1.8.9.1.17.5.1. Con:
            The guards would have almost certainly checked the body was there first, especially considering Pilate's command to "Go, make the tomb as secure as you know how." (Matthew 27:65 [915])

Some modern historians seek to avoid presentism in their work because they consider it a form of cultural bias, and believe it creates a distorted understanding of their subject matter. The practice of presentism is regarded by some as a common fallacy when writing about the past."

[355] https://doi.org/10.1111%2Fbjdp.12164
"Classic research in developmental psychology proposed that children gradually abandon a belief in supernatural causation and instead acquire a more objective and scientific appreciation of cause and effect. According to the secularization hypothesis, with more widespread access to education and technology, natural explanations will increasingly compete with and displace supernatural explanations. In contrast to these hypotheses, there is mounting evidence that individuals across highly diverse cultural contexts use both natural and supernatural explanations to understand the events that occur in their lives. We define natural explanations as those that appeal to ‘empirically verifiable phenomena of the physical or material world’, and supernatural explanations are those that appeal to phenomena that ‘violate, operate outside of, or are distinct from’ the natural world."

"One way to explain the appearances of Jesus Christ was the “twin brother theory.” This hypothesis states that the disciples actually did see someone after Jesus’ death on the cross who looked exactly like Jesus—His identical twin brother. Like magicians who often have twins for assistants, Jesus had a twin brother that no one knew about. […] Response to the Twin Brother Theory

The following things may be said in response to the twin brother theory.

1. There Is No Twin Brother Known

There is no evidence whatsoever that Jesus had a twin brother. The birth accounts in Matthew and Luke make it clear that only one child was born to Joseph and Mary—not identical twins. No record exists anywhere of Jesus having a twin brother.

If such a twin brother of Jesus existed, then why didn’t the religious leader seek him out? Why didn’t they attempt to explain the appearances of Jesus by saying it was a case of mistaken identity? Certainly they would have known about him.

2. …"

"There are some people who believe that it was not actually Jesus Christ who was crucified on the cross of Calvary but rather someone else. Various individuals have been suggested such as Simon of Cyrene, the man who carried Jesus’ cross, or Judas Iscariot. Muslims generally hold this view. They do not believe that one of God’s prophets could be killed in such a cruel way so they assume someone else was actually crucified instead of Jesus.

One who holds such a theory could either conclude that someone else was crucified by mistake or by design. In other words, some sort of plot. Whatever the case may be, it assumes Jesus did not die on the cross of Calvary therefore He did not come back to life three days later.

"According to one of these – a version Dionysius considered ‘rather fabulous’ – Romulus disappeared during a review of the army ‘in the camp’. Suddenly there occurred supernatural phenomena that obscured the light, or somehow removed Romulus from everyone's sight, and when these phenomena ceased, the throne was empty. After searching in vain, the people finally understood – or were made to understand – that the king had ascended to heaven to be among the gods, or better, that he had been swept up into heaven by his father Mars, and therefore was numbered among them."

[519] https://www.magiscenter.com/blog/how-did-shroud-turin-get-image
"According to Jackson, an intense burst of vacuum ultraviolet radiation produced a discoloration on the uppermost surface of the Shroud’s fibrils (without scorching it), which gave rise to a perfect three-dimensional negative image of both the frontal and dorsal parts of the body wrapped in it.”

"We currently do not know of any natural cause for a human corpse producing ultraviolet radiation like this."
[520] https://www.magiscenter.com/blog/what-is-shroud-turin-heres-what-science-says
"The Shroud of Turin is a burial shroud of linen cloth, woven in a 3:1 herringbone pattern. Measuring 14 feet 3 inches in length and 3 feet 7 inches in width, it is a single piece of cloth bearing a front and back image of a crucified man.

The Shroud has been venerated as the Burial cloth of Jesus since at least 1355 when it was displayed for the first time in Lirey, France. 

The Shroud has undergone more scientific testing than any relic in human history. These investigations concern the unique nature of the image and the dating of the cloth itself."
[521] https://www.magiscenter.com/blog/how-did-shroud-turin-get-image
"The formation of the Shroud’s image would take several billion watts of light radiation, which exceeds the maximum output of any source of UV radiation known today. If the accompanying heat energy had been present, the cloth would have vaporized in less than 1/40 billionth of a second.

Jackson’s theory was validated in 2010 by Paoli DiLazzaro and his team who concluded,

“In particular, vacuum ultraviolet photons account for the very thin coloration depth, the hue of color and the presence of image in linen parts not in contact with the body. Obviously, it does not mean the image was produced by a laser. Rather, the laser is a powerful tool to test and obtain the light parameters suitable for a shroud-like coloration.”"
[522] https://www.magiscenter.com/blog/facts-about-shroud-turin
[523] https://www.magiscenter.com/blog/verified-scientific-facts-supporting-the-authenticity-of-the-shroud-of-turin
[524] https://www.magiscenter.com/blog/the-shroud-of-turin-inspires-a-hyper-realistic-body-of-christ
"With the back being slightly raised, you can see the wounds inflicted by the crown of thorns. Just below the head, bruises cover the back to depict the weight of the cross He bore.

On the face, the right eye is bruised and the nose is broken to show the torture endured. The hands and the feet show the gruesome trauma of the nails that pierced them and, between the fifth and sixth rib on the right side, the wound inflicted by the spear."

"There are four other dating tests performed on fibers from the Shroud:

A vanillin test by Dr. Raymond Rogers.

Two spectroscopic analyses (of Dr. Giulio Fanti, et al.)

The compressibility and breaking strength tests (of Dr. Giulio Fanti, et al.).

The results indicate a midpoint average of 50 A.D. (plus or minus 200 years) with a 96% confidence level."
[529] https://www.magiscenter.com/blog/how-old-is-shroud-turin

[532] https://www.magiscenter.com/blog/facts-about-shroud-turin
"Blood is rare AB type+ (same type as found on the Sudarium—see next question below).

Blood plasma around the blood stains is revealed under UV light.

Stains match descriptions of the Passion of Jesus in the Gospels.

Blood particles reveal a high content of bilirubin. Significant for two reasons:

Consistent with bodily response to extreme trauma (so a dead body could not have been used to create the image).

Blood with high bilirubin content stays red over time and does not turn dark brown (consistent with stains on the Shroud)."

"Jewish Christians continued to observe Shabbat but met together at the end of the day, on a Saturday evening... Although Christians meeting for worship on the first day of the week (Sunday for Gentiles) dates back to Acts and is historically mentioned around 115 AD, Constantine's edict was the start of many more Christians observing only Sunday and not the Sabbath.[14] Patristic writings attest that by the second century, it had become commonplace to celebrate the Eucharist in a corporate day of worship on the first day.[16] A Church Father, Eusebius, who became the bishop of Caesarea Maritima about AD 314, stated that for Christians, "the sabbath had been transferred to Sunday"."

[560] https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistle_to_Titus
"Titus, along with the two other pastoral epistles (1 Timothy and 2 Timothy), is regarded by some scholars as being pseudepigraphical.[6] On the basis of the language and content of the pastoral epistles, these scholars reject that they were written by Paul and believe that they were written by an anonymous forger after his death. Critics claim the vocabulary and style of the Pauline letters could not have been written by Paul according to available biographical information and reflect the views of the emerging Church rather than the apostle's. These scholars date the epistle from the 80s CE up to the end of the 2nd century, though most would place it sometime between 80 and 100 CE.[7] The Church of England's Common Worship Lectionary Scripture Commentary concurs with this view: "the proportioning of the theological and practical themes is one factor that leads us to think of these writings as coming from the post-Pauline church world of the late first or early second century"."

"Proskynesis (Greek προσκύνησις, proskýnēsis; Latin adoratio) is a solemn gesture of respect for the gods and people; among the Persians, it referred to a man prostrating himself and kissing the earth, or the limbs of a respected person.
